Introduction
Purpose
This report provides a comprehensive overview of the current humanitarian situation, focusing on both operational environment
factors and sectoral analysis. It aims to support informed decision-making for humanitarian partners and stakeholders.
About GANNET
GANNET is a Generative AI Initiative from Data Friendly Space, offering AI-powered tools built to make knowledge and real-time
information accessible to our community in a resource-efficient and ethical way. It provides tools such as the GANNET Virtual Assistant
and GANNET SituationHub to help humanitarian actors access, analyze, and utilize critical information for their operations.
GANNET SituationHub
The GANNET SituationHub is a generative AI-powered digital public good designed to identify and analyze humanitarian data from
thousands of trusted sources in real time. By transforming complex data into clear insights, it enables faster, more effective response
strategies and improved coordination among stakeholders.
GANNET Virtual Assistant
The GANNET Virtual Assistant is a verified chatbot designed for humanitarian quick decision-making. Built on cutting-edge Generative
AI and trained with humanitarian expert-reviewed data, it provides real-time information. The GANNET Virtual Assistant facilitates rapid
data access and synthesis for crisis response.
Methodology Overview
The Situation Hub's analytical process uses a structured Human-in-the-Loop workflow that combines automated analysis retrieval
through GANNET Virtual Assistant, DFS's AI-powered humanitarian assistant, with expert review by the DFS analysis team. GANNET
automates the extraction and generation of crisis-related insights based on structured prompts, which are aligned with a tailored
analytical framework. These outputs are then reviewed, validated, and refined by analysts to ensure accuracy, contextual relevance,
and operational utility. The workflow is organized into four key phases: planning and setup, ongoing operations, quality assurance, and
system evolution, ensuring a balance between speed and analytical rigor.
Planning, Setup & Quality Control
In the planning phase, analysts develop a contextualized analytical framework, largely based on the Joint Intersectoral Analysis
Framework (JIAF), and convert it into structured prompts for AI-assisted data extraction. These prompts are refined over time to
improve output quality. Data sources are vetted for reliability, and translations are reviewed for accuracy. Using a centralized Content
Management System (CMS), analysts review and curate AI-generated outputs, validate them against external sources, and make
refinements to enhance clarity and relevance. The CMS enables collaborative editing and version tracking, ensuring outputs align with
humanitarian needs.
Bias Mitigation
To minimize bias, the team diversifies data sources, cross-verifies information, and continuously evaluates the AI's performance.
Analysts are crucial in identifying and correcting inaccuracies, especially in automated categorization. Future improvements aim to
enhance source credibility checks and sentiment analysis, further strengthening the reliability of the Situation Hub's outputs.
Crisis Overview
The SituationHub landing pages use a standardized yet customizable template to deliver consistent, comprehensive crisis overviews.
These pages integrate static content with dynamic data from sources like ACLED and DTM via open APIs, enabling interactive visuals
with real-time updates. Regular reviews and verification with in-country partners ensure accuracy, while manual updates are used for
sources not connected to APIs.
Media Monitoring
The media monitoring process has evolved from a manual, list-based approach to an AI-assisted system integrated into the CMS. This
allows analysts to efficiently review, filter, and contextualize media narratives, while automation helps track emerging trends.
Disclaimer
This tool is provided by SCI oPt CO and DFS and generates content using GANNET, following a multisectoral humanitarian analysis
framework widely adopted and refined for this project. The following content is produced by GANNET under the guidance of
humanitarian analysts, and every output is carefully reviewed and refined by experts to ensure it reflects humanitarian principles,
contextual understanding, and data accuracy before being shared. Please be aware that the information may still include gaps or
inaccuracies and is intended for informational purposes only. It does not constitute official advice or necessarily reflect the official
views or messaging of SCI or DFS.
4
Overview
The following overview has been generated using the information available up to June 9, 2026. It provides a synthesized summary and
key insights into the crisis based on the most recent data accessible at that time.
Summary
The Occupied Palestinian Territory is facing a severe humanitarian crisis, with both Gaza and the West Bank experiencing
unprecedented levels of violence and deprivation. In Gaza, following the collapse of a brief pause in hostilities in March 2025, Israeli
forces resumed intensive bombardment while maintaining a complete blockade that has now entered its eighth week, preventing all
humanitarian aid and commercial supplies from entering. This has left Gaza's 2.2 million residents facing starvation as food stocks
deplete, bakeries cease operations due to lack of flour and fuel, and nearly 90% of the population remains displaced with critical
infrastructure almost entirely destroyed. A new U.S.-backed peace plan led to a pause in hostilities agreement coming into effect on 10
October 2025, raising cautious hopes for increased humanitarian access and a potential reduction in hostilities after months of
relentless violence. However, despite the pause in hostilities, sporadic violations, limited aid entry, and continued displacement
highlight the fragility of the pause in hostilities and the deep humanitarian strain that persists across Gaza.
Meanwhile, the West Bank has seen a dramatic escalation in military operations, particularly since the launch of "Operation Iron Wall"
in January 2025, which has resulted in mass displacement of Palestinians and widespread destruction across refugee camps. Israeli
forces have displaced approximately 40,000 people, with officials stating that residents will not be allowed to return for at least a year,
while expanded checkpoints and military presence severely restrict movement between Palestinian cities. 
International organisations and UN bodies have expressed increasing concern about systematic violations of international
humanitarian law in the West Bank, including accelerating settlement expansion, land reclassification and forced displacement that
multiple observers warn are driving de facto annexation dynamics. These concerns have intensified following the International Court of
Justice's 2024 advisory opinion on the illegality of the prolonged occupation and the Israeli authorities' security cabinet decisions of 8
February 2026 expanding civil and administrative control over West Bank land and governance.
Key Insights
1. Gaza has entered famine conditions, with projections suggesting the crisis could spread to central and southern areas by
September, affecting up to 640,000 people. Between May and September 2025, 470,000 people (22% of the population) were in
Catastrophe/IPC Phase 5, over 1 million in Emergency/Phase 4, and the rest in Crisis/Phase 3—meaning the entire population faces
severe food insecurity. By the end of September, famine conditions were expected to extend beyond Gaza City into Deir al-Balah and
Khan Yunis. Humanitarian aid remains far below needs due to restrictions on access and delivery, driving acute malnutrition, especially
among children, while health and nutrition services collapse under the strain.
2. Over the 2025 April–March period, ~71,000 children under five are projected to suffer from acute malnutrition, with ~14,100 of these
being severe cases. Similarly, nearly 17,000 pregnant and breastfeeding women are expected to require treatment for acute
malnutrition. The rapid rise of malnutrition in children, especially in Gaza City is a red flag: it both signals and drives higher mortality
risks.
3. In the West Bank, "Operation Iron Wall" launched in January 2025 has led to the largest forced displacement since 1967, with
approximately 40,000 Palestinians displaced from refugee camps including Jenin, Tulkarem, Nur Shams, and El Far\'a, with Israeli
officials stating residents cannot return for at least a year.
4. Israeli forces deployed tanks in the northern West Bank in February 2025 for the first time in 20 years, while establishing at least 20
new iron gates and numerous checkpoints throughout the territory, severely restricting movement between Palestinian cities and
villages.
5. According to World Bank assessments (February 2025), reconstruction and recovery needs in Gaza require approximately US$53
billion, with housing accounting for 53% of damages, while commerce and industry represent 20%, and critical infrastructure including
health, water, and transport comprising 15%.
6. The collapse of local food systems is near total , 98% of cropland in Gaza is reported as destroyed or inaccessible, and fishing has
been banned or heavily restricted. Because of wartime blockades and displacement, the normal supply chains for food, fuel, water,
and medical supplies have been disrupted or severed. In Gaza City and Khan Yunis, wheat flour prices have reportedly jumped ~3,000%
compared to earlier months. Many households are resorting to extreme coping strategies: selling clothes, foraging or collecting trash,
or going whole days without food.
5
Operational Environment
Economy
Current Economic Situation
Gaza's economy has entered a complex collapse driven by the cessation of trade, closure of crossings, destruction of markets and
factories, liquidity shortages, and sharp price surges that have drained savings and livelihoods [2]. 
Humanitarian partners report elevated but generally stable prices, with fresh products most volatile, and cash actors are calling for
increased commercial truck access and fee reductions to support market recovery [10]. Kerem Shalom and Zikim remained the only
operational entry points for humanitarian and commercial goods, with an offloading rate of 81% across all corridors between 11–17 May,
but only about half of aid trucks from Egypt were able to offload at Israeli crossings in the first 18 days of May, constraining supply flows
[10][3]. Between 7–20 May, UNOPS delivered more than 2.1 million litres of diesel into Gaza and distributed just over 2 million litres to
support humanitarian operations, underscoring critical fuel dependence for basic services [10].
The World Food Programme estimated about 1.6 million people in Gaza (approximately 77% of the population) face high levels of acute
food insecurity [9]. The UN's 2026 flash appeal sought $4 billion for 3 million Palestinians but had received only approximately 12%
(about $490 million), leaving a major funding shortfall that is hampering relief operations [9][41]. 
UNRWA and nutrition partners reported a 21% increase in malnutrition admissions in January–April 2026 compared with the same
period in 2025 [8]. 
The World Bank estimated reconstruction costs for Gaza at about $71 billion, while international proposals and UN resolution language
linking reconstruction financing to security measures and Palestinian governance reform have raised concerns about conditionality
and risks of prolonged external trusteeship [1][6]. 
On 26 May 2026, Prime Minister Mohammad Mustafa urged international partners to resume UNRWA funding and for Israel to release
Palestinian tax revenues withheld on its behalf to alleviate the fiscal squeeze and support essential services and reconstruction
planning [12].
Conflict Economic Landscape
Gaza's civilian infrastructure suffered extreme damage, with reports indicating around 90% destroyed, and Israeli control of crossings
and large parts of Gaza limits the movement of goods and materials, constraining reconstruction and recovery [55][62]. 
Humanitarian access targets of 600 aid trucks per day are not being met, and aid deliveries frequently offload far below planned
volumes, worsening shortages of fuel, medical supplies, and reconstruction materials [13][15][6]. 
Displacement is severe, with up to 1.9 million of 2.3 million people internally displaced and roughly 60% of the population estimated to
have lost their homes [39][3].
Agricultural production has been devastated: the Palestinian Ministry of Agriculture estimates more than $4 billion in sector losses with
over 80% of agricultural land damaged, and the FAO reports under 5% of Gaza's agricultural land remains usable [22][39]. 
The health system has collapsed, with dialysis access for approximately 250 patients at risk, around 11,000 people facing insulin
shortages, haemophilia patients without treatment, and the destruction of much medical imaging capacity—all nine MRI units lost and
only five of 18 CT scanners functioning [3]. 
WASH and sanitation conditions are critical, with severe shortages of engine oil (estimated at approximately 7,000 litres/month),
shutdowns of critical stormwater infrastructure to preserve generators, and sanitary landfills remaining inaccessible, driving pest
outbreaks and public health risks [10][15].
Reconstruction financing remains largely unrealized. Palestinian authorities are seeking international cooperation, development-bank
support, and public-private partnerships, including talks with the World Bank, to mobilize reconstruction finance, but major pledged
funds have not materialized: the Board of Peace's World Bank-administered Gaza fund reported no donor deposits, and commentators
note only about 1% of the roughly $17 billion reportedly pledged had been transferred [17][62][63][3]. 
Over 30 reconstruction proposals have emerged since October 2023, but they lack coordinated funding, and several analyses warn
that plans under-prioritize health, education, and social recovery alongside physical rebuilding [10][1][17]. 
Operational constraints further hamper recovery, as debris-removal machinery, landfill access, and other critical inputs remain
blocked or restricted by security and administrative measures [10][1]. Regional disparities are evident: Gaza faces near-total physical
and economic collapse, while the West Bank endures rising unemployment, movement restrictions, and pressures from settlement
expansion that are reshaping local economic and planning realities [7][57].
Stability of Currency and Bank System
Gaza's economy is suffering declining liquidity, weak market resilience, and severe restrictions on crossings and aid deliveries, all of
which are constraining commercial activity and reconstruction prospects [2][6][3]. 
The Palestinian Authority has struggled to pay public-sector salaries after Israel withheld large portions of Palestinian tax revenues,
leaving workers with billions of dollars in unpaid wages by mid-2025, according to the World Bank [7]. 
Reconstruction financing and international aid are also being linked to political and security conditions in post-war planning, including
disarmament requirements, complicating prospects for rapid economic recovery and reliable external funding flows [6].
6
Employment
Reporting in late May 2026 indicates a collapsed jobs market across the occupied Palestinian territory, with unemployment having
more than doubled since October 2023, peaking at 35.2% in early 2024 and standing at 27.5% by end-2025 [7]. Nearly 40% of young
Palestinians in the occupied West Bank holding at least a diploma are unemployed, according to the Palestine Economic Policy
Research Institute (MAS) [7]. Israel indefinitely froze work permits for about 115,000 West Bank workers, with only a few permits
subsequently renewed, compounding unemployment in the West Bank [7]. The Palestinian Authority's public sector has become an
increasingly unreliable source of stable employment, with public-sector workers accumulating billions of dollars in unpaid wages by
mid-2025, prompting many to avoid government jobs [7]. In Gaza, widespread destruction of markets, businesses, and factories,
combined with blockade-related trade and cash-flow disruptions, forced hundreds of thousands to lose their income and produced
an unprecedented labour-market collapse [2].
Humanitarian operations have also cut local jobs: World Central Kitchen dismissed more than 500 employees in Gaza and reduced
daily cooked meals from 1,000,000 to 200,000, directly reducing employment in food-assistance operations [9]. Palestinian authorities
are seeking to link reconstruction with job creation through international cooperation, development banks, and public-private
partnerships, but reconstruction plans face major funding shortfalls and delays [17][1].
Politics
Governance Structures and Rule of Law
Since the adoption of UN Resolution 2803 (2025), an unprecedented international governance architecture has been built for Gaza,
including a Peace Council reportedly chaired by President Donald Trump, an international stabilization force, a transitional
technocratic administration, and periodic six-monthly reports to the Security Council [123]. The Secretary-General's report
(S/2026/418) and the Mladenov briefing make reconstruction financing conditional on security measures, explicitly linking international
funding to Hamas disarmament and Palestinian Authority reform [123]. Humanitarian and damage figures cited in this context include
that about 80% of Gaza's buildings were destroyed or damaged, some 70 million tonnes of rubble were generated, more than 2 million
people were described as in catastrophic conditions, and the United Nations requested a $3.72 billion emergency appeal in December
2025 [123].
Critics warn that new aid structures and reconstruction arrangements — including the Gaza Humanitarian Foundation and the
US-backed "Board of Peace" — and their security linkages risk depoliticizing the Palestinian question, undermining aid neutrality, and
converting relief into instruments of population management rather than addressing rights and self-determination [170]. Palestinian
leaders and analysts have expressed concern that externally imposed bodies such as a proposed Gaza Management Committee and
other trustee-style arrangements threaten the PLO's role and risk marginalizing established Palestinian national representation
[135][137]. The strategy is being pursued across three explicit tracks — transitional governance, security restructuring, and political
engineering — with identified risks including erosion of sovereignty, potential administrative separation of Gaza from the wider
Palestinian polity, and the rise of technocratic actors prioritised for service delivery over popular representation [123].
In the West Bank, a draft Israeli law and related heritage initiatives emphasising "Jewish heritage" have been criticised as tools for
incremental annexation by expanding Israeli intervention over planning, land, and antiquities while curtailing Palestinian Authority
powers [166].
Political Freedoms and Recent Laws
UN briefings — notably the Mladenov briefing (21 May 2026) and the Peace Council report S/2026/418 — link post-war reconstruction to
security arrangements and condition international funding on Hamas disarmament, a framing Palestinians say turns reconstruction
from a right into a political lever [123]. Resolution 2803 is cited as formalising this shift by requiring state reform and coupling financing
to security benchmarks, raising concerns about the emergence of trusteeship-style management and the structural separation of
Gaza from national Palestinian institutions [123]. Analyses warn the post-war policy trajectory may prioritise technocratic or
transitional administrations focused on service delivery and stability over representative political legitimacy [123].
In late May 2026, UN reporting indicated Israel was to be added to the UN list of parties credibly suspected of conflict-related sexual
violence, and Israel announced it would sever ties with the Secretary-General's office in response [190][197][196]. Observers noted that
the suspension of direct engagement with UN investigators — including cancellation of proposed visits — undermines channels for
judicial and fact-finding processes and may limit accountability pathways [193]. A WHO resolution defining Gaza as occupied and
mandating monitoring of health impacts was adopted, but critics argue such resolutions have not led to effective accountability or
changed conditions on the ground [195]. Reports also noted the withdrawal of Palestinian Permanent Representative Riad Mansour's
candidacy for UN General Assembly Vice-President, reportedly under US pressure [129].
Influence of Non-State Actors and Political Factions
Palestinian resistance factions have publicly rejected the Mladenov proposals, characterising the linkage of aid, fuel, and
reconstruction to disarmament as political and humanitarian blackmail that deepens internal division and risks separating Gaza from
the broader Palestinian national project [199][201]. Israeli political calculations — including tying ceasefire implementation and
administrative deployment to the disarmament agenda and reported stalling of ceasefire commitments — further complicate efforts
to decouple reconstruction from security-driven political outcomes [198][123].
The European Union has expanded sanctions on Hamas' Politburo and stated that disarmament is an essential prerequisite for
progress, reflecting international policy pressure that ties security conditions to political and reconstruction processes [167]. Observers
warn that new aid structures and initiatives risk politicising assistance, undermining humanitarian neutrality, and making aid delivery
an instrument of external influence and control [170].
7
The Palestinian Centre for Political Studies (May 2026) called for a specialised Palestinian position room to follow international
arrangements and reiterated that Gaza is inseparable from a comprehensive national political issue [123].
Public Trust in State Institutions
Analysts warn that the reconstruction-security linkage risks reframing reconstruction from a human right into a bargaining chip,
reshaping political legitimacy, and deepening internal divisions — developments collectively described as producing a deep strategic
crisis of confidence over the prospects for a stable political transition in Gaza [123]. The Mladenov briefing and the Secretary-General's
report S/2026/418 are described as signalling a shift from ending the war to managing stability, creating an implementation gap and
deepening Palestinian strategic distrust [123].
Observers note the Palestine Liberation Organization has been politically marginalised since Oslo and call for its renewal, including
convening a new Palestinian National Council to rebuild representative legitimacy — reflecting internal concerns about weakened
national institutions and authority [135]. At WUF13, Palestinian Minister Sami Hijawi called for stronger global support for rebuilding,
reported sharply rising unemployment linked to the war, and said Palestinian authorities are in talks with international institutions
including the World Bank to expand financial support for reconstruction [132].
Role of Marginalized Groups
The PLO Executive Committee reaffirmed commitment to democratic renewal and to completing preparations for Palestinian National
Council elections and institutional reform before the end of the year, emphasizing inclusion of political forces, civil society, youth, and
women to rebuild representation and legitimacy [159][135]. Palestinian analysts have warned that the current reconstruction
framework risks redefining political legitimacy toward technocratic "management," turning reconstruction into a tool of political
pressure, and marginalising broader segments of Palestinian society — including youth, women, and civil society — from meaningful
participation in shaping their political future [123].
Security And Stability
Security Overview
UN Secretary-General António Guterres warned that civilian protection deteriorated in 2025, identifying the Occupied Palestinian
Territory among conflicts with particularly high civilian harm and noting at least 20,000 Palestinians were killed or their remains
recovered in Gaza during 2025 [214]. Security Council and UN briefings in late May 2026 highlighted continued ceasefire violations,
ongoing Israeli strikes in Gaza, and warnings about a potential expansion of operations into Lebanon that risk further escalation and
civilian harm [237][276]. OCHA and UN reporting described Gaza's humanitarian conditions as critical—scarce water, collapsed
services, and near-daily attacks driving mass displacement and high civilian casualties since the October 2023 offensive [276]. UN and
Peace Council documents (S/2026/418) warn that the continued armed presence of Hamas and other factions hinders safe,
predictable restoration of services in Gaza, and that reconstruction is being linked to disarmament and security arrangements, posing
risks of political and security re-engineering in the enclave [213].
Humanitarian operations face acute security threats globally and in Gaza specifically: more than 325 aid workers were killed globally in
2025, attacks on health care surged—including a marked increase in armed-drone strikes and follow-up "double-tap" incidents—and
emergency medical response in Gaza has been severely degraded by recurrent strikes and restricted movements [214][216][227]. UN
sources also reported restricted access for independent monitors and the International Committee of the Red Cross to certain facilities
in the Occupied Palestinian Territory, limiting verification and accountability efforts [222][252].
Security Forces and Law Enforcement
The UN Secretary-General's annual report placed Israeli military, prison, and security services on its blacklist for conflict-related sexual
violence, with UN investigators verifying 31 cases involving 14 men, 7 women, 9 boys, and 1 girl—13 incidents occurring in 2025 and 18
dating back to 2023–2024 [228][229][252][268][222]. The report attributed these abuses to members of the Israel Defence Forces, the
Israel Prison Service (including Keter), and police counter-terrorism units such as Yamam, documenting violations including rape,
gang rape, targeted genital violence, forced nudity, and degrading strip/cavity searches [228][229]. Israeli authorities denied or
restricted access for UN investigators and the ICRC to detention facilities and had not demonstrated compliance with preventive
measures under Security Council resolution 2467; the UN also warned that the dropping of charges against five reserve soldiers in the
Sde Teiman case in March 2026 could entrench impunity [228][229][225]. Palestinian authorities and protection actors welcomed the
UN listing as recognition of systemic abuses and called for accountability and prosecution of perpetrators [261][246].
In the West Bank, Israeli forces' operations, extensive movement restrictions—including an estimated 930 checkpoints and gates—and
escalating settler violence are heightening protection risks; OCHA documented more than 50 attacks in one week and an average of
six settler attacks per day in 2026, constraining Palestinians' access to services and undermining local protection mechanisms
[218][259]. In Gaza, UNMAS conducted explosive-hazard assessments and risk-education activities and recorded 109 accidents since
the October 2025 ceasefire, resulting in 265 injured and 49 killed, underscoring gaps in clearance capacity and residual security risks
for civilians and responders alike [218][221]. Humanitarian partners also reported severe operational constraints, noting partially
functional VHF communications and that only half of aid trucks from Egypt offloaded at Kerem Shalom in the first 18 days of May,
limiting responders' ability to coordinate and reducing effective law-enforcement presence to ensure civilian protection [218].
8
Demography
Demographic Overview
The Gaza Strip's population is reported at roughly 2.1–2.3 million people [294][291][310]. Massive internal displacement characterizes
the demographic picture, with approximately 1.9 million of 2.3 million Palestinians internally displaced, many forced to flee multiple
times, and humanitarian partners describing nearly the entire population as uprooted and sheltering in makeshift settings [291][293].
OCHA reported approximately 1.7 million people sheltering in around 1,600 displacement sites, while partners recorded new
displacement of more than 150 families between 16–17 May 2026 — 40 of whom required immediate emergency assistance — and
returnee assistance figures reached 2,429 people following Rafah's reopening as of 14 May [300][296][292].
By late May 2026, Israeli forces effectively controlled about 60–64% of Gaza, and on 28 May Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu stated
he had directed the military to expand that control to 70%. Humanitarian agencies and UN bodies warn that the remaining civilian
population is increasingly compressed into a much smaller coastal area — commonly cited as roughly 30–40% of the Strip, with some
local actors describing even smaller habitable pockets — intensifying overcrowding and displacement pressures
[323][317][294][295][289]. Political statements and policy measures signaling planned population movement, including Israeli
announcements of a "voluntary migration" scheme and the creation of a Defence-linked body to manage departures, have been
repeatedly reported and criticized by rights bodies as measures that would produce permanent demographic change or forcible
displacement if implemented [294][309][310].
The destruction of civilian infrastructure is extensive: UNICEF reported more than 97% of schools damaged or destroyed, leaving
approximately 658,000 children without formal education for over two years, and UN agencies have documented widespread
functional collapse of basic services [291][295]. Food and health vulnerabilities are widespread, with the World Food Programme
reporting about 1.6 million people facing high levels of acute food insecurity, compounded by large-scale cuts to emergency food
operations — including an 80% reduction in World Central Kitchen's cooked meals affecting hundreds of thousands of displaced
households [305].
Socio-Cultural
Socio-Cultural Overview
OCHA's May 25, 2026 humanitarian situation report documented escalating settler violence, Israeli forces' operations, demolitions,
displacement, and movement restrictions across the West Bank that are heightening protection risks and disrupting community
cohesion [352]. Hundreds of Palestinians in Area C of eastern Jerusalem face imminent forced displacement after the Israeli Finance
Minister instructed rapid implementation of long-standing demolition orders against Khan al-Ahmar — one of 18 Bedouin and herding
communities affecting about 4,000 people — prompting high levels of fear and uncertainty among residents [361][352]. Since 2009,
roughly 550 structures in these 18 communities were demolished for lacking Israeli permits, including 175 donor-funded humanitarian
structures, undermining livelihoods and communal belonging [352]. During the reporting period, OCHA documented more than 40
raids and other operations by Israeli forces across the West Bank — including house searches, mass detentions, and temporary home
evacuations — which disrupt access to services and erode trust between communities and authorities [352].
A sharp rise in settler attacks (more than 50 in a single week) brought the 2026 total to some 870 incidents across 220+ communities,
with incidents such as arson, destruction of olive trees, cutting electricity, and physical assaults deepening intercommunal tensions
and insecurity [351]. Israeli moves over cultural and religious sites — including a draft law reframing West Bank antiquities to
emphasise a "Jewish heritage" narrative and an order to seize the Prophet Samuel shrine — are perceived by Palestinians and rights
groups as instruments of creeping annexation that threaten Palestinian identity, historical memory, and social cohesion [355][410].
Challenges to the custodianship of Al-Aqsa and to Jordanian Waqf guardianship risk inflaming religious and national sensitivities
across Palestinian and Jordanian communities in late May 2026 [370]. Restrictions on religious life extend to Gaza, where authorities
blocked residents from performing the Hajj for the third consecutive year, reinforcing grievances linked to freedom of worship and
community dignity [351].
Repeated and often multiple displacements in Gaza are dismantling normal social life — overcrowded shelters, loss of livelihoods,
interrupted education, and rising protection risks for women, children, and older people — straining communal coping mechanisms
and social networks [367][352]. Humanitarian and analytical sources warn that pressure on services and contested aid delivery
weaken local governance and social structures, increase dependence on external assistance, and make access to aid itself a source
of tension and power within and between Palestinian communities [340][357].
Gender Dynamics
On 29 May 2026, the United Nations added Israeli entities to its annex of parties credibly implicated in conflict-related sexual violence,
a designation welcomed by the Palestinian authorities [405][373]. UN investigators documented 31 verified cases involving 14 men, 7
women, 9 boys, and 1 girl, with 13 cases occurring in 2025 and 18 in 2023–24 [359]. The UN report highlighted a systemic lack of
accountability, including the dropping of charges in March 2026 against five reserve soldiers linked to a severe assault at Sde Teiman
and continued denial of UN access to detention facilities for investigation [359][360].
The Palestinian Ministry of Women's Affairs warned that ongoing occupation, blockade, displacement, and restrictions have increased
risks to women and girls — including sexual violence, exploitation, extortion, and exclusion — and called for international action to
ensure protection and accountability [363]. Humanitarian sources report acute menstrual-hygiene shortages in Gaza, with nearly
700,000 women and girls lacking basic sanitary pads and facing unsafe, undignified conditions in shelters [378]. Local women's
organisations continue protection and capacity efforts, for example through a legal awareness session for 15 women in Ain Arik on 12
May 2026 aimed at improving access to legal protection and services [341].
9
UN Women and related analyses have emphasised that women-led and women's rights organisations are underfunded and need
flexible, multi-year support to deliver protection, GBV services, and participation in decision-making during the crisis [345].
Impact of Socio-Cultural Drivers on Aid
Cultural norms and social dynamics shape both the vulnerabilities communities face and the ways in which humanitarian assistance
is delivered and received. The acute shortage of menstrual hygiene supplies — with nearly 700,000 women and girls in Gaza lacking
basic sanitary pads and forced to use improvised cloth, face long queues for toilets, and lack safe disposal options — undermines
dignity and the cultural acceptability of humanitarian responses [378][377]. Restricted crossings, active hostilities, and damaged
infrastructure further constrain delivery and repair of culturally and technically specific items such as WASH spare parts and sanitary
supplies, limiting the reach and appropriateness of assistance and increasing reliance on local coping mechanisms [367][381].
Displacement has disrupted social networks but also triggered informal coping strategies — families sharing food, women organising
shelters, youth volunteering — and local initiatives such as the Aida Camp bazaar have reinforced community cohesion and provided
economic opportunities [367][399]. UNICEF's 2026 Gaza WASH response supported around 1 million people with water, nearly 509,000
with sanitation, and over 603,300 with critical hygiene supplies, and piloted household waste segregation in four shelters that reduced
waste by about 15–20% [381]. UNICEF is also strengthening accountability to affected people through accessible information,
community participation, and confidential feedback mechanisms to adapt programming [381], though its 2026 Humanitarian Action
for Children appeal of US$707.8 million remains only 36% funded, with an outstanding gap of US$451.8 million [381]. Protection partners
conducted monitoring through eight focus group discussions and 130 key informant interviews across 16 neighbourhoods (1,155
people), and between 11–17 May provided mental health and psychosocial support to over 4,000 children and about 1,500 caregivers
while delivering community-based protection activities reaching another 4,700 children and caregivers and managing hundreds of
child protection cases [361].
Environment
Environmental Conditions
Gaza's environmental conditions are shaped by acute water insecurity, climate hazards, and collapsing infrastructure. UNICEF reported
that some 54 partners provide roughly 24,000 cubic metres of water per day to people in more than 2,000 locations, with about 74% of
households relying on these deliveries (late May 2026) [434][444]. 
Despite this, most families remain water-insecure (82%), with up to 70% unable to collect the minimum 6 litres per person per day for
drinking and cooking [428][451][444]. Seawater desalination output declined from approximately 20,000 m³/day in March to 16,000
m³/day due to shortages of chemicals and spare parts [428][451][444].
Climatic hazards compound these pressures significantly. Approximately 800,000 people — nearly 40% of Gaza's population — live in
flood-prone sites, and heavy storms in March turned streets into stagnant lakes and damaged shelters for more than 3,000 displaced
people [429][437][425]. 
A heatwave last summer pushed temperatures above 40°C, elevating risks of dehydration and food spoilage for displaced
populations with limited electricity or shelter protection [429][437][428]. Rising temperatures, collapsed sewage systems, and
accumulating waste have driven rodent and insect infestations, heightening risks of diarrhoeal disease, hepatitis A, and skin infections
in overcrowded, electricity-deprived displacement settings [429][437][428].
Environmental Challenges
The conflict has generated severe and compounding environmental challenges across water, sanitation, waste management, and
public health. Humanitarian reporting highlights critical strain on sanitation and wastewater systems, with sewage overflows, limited
wastewater treatment functionality, and restricted access to managed landfills; partners have prioritised requests for reverse-osmosis
units, water-system spare parts, solid-waste compactors, and emergency repair kits for pipelines to prevent sudden service collapse
[444][451]. 
Water-trucking operations at the Al Mansoura filling point were suspended following the killing of two contracted drivers on 17 April,
forcing partners to truck an additional 2 million litres per day from desalination plants at an estimated extra cost of approximately USD
40,000 per day to cover 285,000 people [428][451].
UNICEF reported alarming accumulation of solid waste around displacement sites, with temporary dumps near southern Gaza nearing
capacity and rising rodent and pest infestations worsening environmental health risks for displaced families [428][429]. 
Partners reported rising trends in skin diseases and acute watery and bloody diarrhoea, while environmental surveillance for
March–April 2026 found no poliovirus detection across 12 consecutive months, though a rodent-associated disease risk assessment
remains ongoing — underscoring immediate public-health threats linked to WASH and waste failures [444].
The broader environmental footprint of the conflict has also been quantified: new research estimates the Israel–Gaza war has
generated approximately 33 million tonnes of CO2e to date, with emissions from active military operations alone exceeding 1.3 million
tonnes, adding a long-term climate dimension to the crisis [437][429][426].
Environmental Risks and Mitigation
Key near-term risks centre on WASH system failures and flood exposure. Severe shortages of engine and lubricating oil — estimated at
about 7,000 litres per month and required to operate water and wastewater systems — have curtailed life-saving WASH activities and
prompted the shutdown of the Sheikh Radwan stormwater lagoon on 11 May, raising near-term flood risk for the roughly 800,000
people living in flood-prone areas [425][444][424][451][437]. 
10
Accumulating solid waste, inaccessible sanitary landfills, and damaged sanitation infrastructure have driven rodent and insect
infestations linked to rising public-health risks, including diarrhoeal disease, skin infections, and hepatitis A [428][444][425].
In response, UN agencies and partners have delivered three tonnes of rodenticides and 3,000 litres of pesticides and launched
pest-control operations across more than 1,700 locations; however, they warn that a full response requires restored access to sanitary
landfills and the entry of debris-removal machinery, trucks, and compactors [424][434][444]. 
Humanitarian experts and NGOs are calling for climate adaptation to be integrated into emergency response, prioritising resilient
WASH systems, strengthened disease surveillance, and expanded sustainable energy — such as solar power — to reduce
environmental and public-health impacts in the medium term [426][437][425].
Conflict
Overview of the Ongoing Armed Conflict
The conflict intensified after Hamas's 7 October 2023 attack and the subsequent major Israeli military campaign in Gaza, evolving into
a prolonged, regionally escalating confrontation by mid-2026 [546][579]. Active operations are concentrated in the Gaza Strip, where
Israel is reported to control roughly 60–70% of the territory and continues targeted strikes and territorial expansion, while the occupied
West Bank faces rising settler violence and Israeli raids, and southern Lebanon has seen cross-border Israeli–Hezbollah exchanges
and strikes in late May 2026 [524][538][543][531][573]. 
Despite a ceasefire declared in October 2025, strikes, raids and targeted killings have persisted into late May 2026, including Israeli
operations that killed newly appointed Hamas commanders [524][579][546].
Gaza health authorities report cumulative casualties since October 2023 of over 72,900 killed and approximately 172,900 injured; since
the October 2025 ceasefire, roughly 929 have been killed and 2,811 injured from subsequent operations, indicating sustained high
lethality [579][525]. The conflict has devastated critical infrastructure, with approximately 90% of civilian infrastructure in Gaza
damaged, UN estimates of roughly USD 70 billion needed for reconstruction, and widespread destruction of hospitals, water and
electricity networks resulting in a near-collapse of the health system [582][513][523].
Humanitarian access remains severely constrained: OCHA and partners report movement restrictions, requirements to coordinate
with Israeli authorities, recurrent strikes on populated areas, and aid-throughput targets of 600 trucks per day being met at far lower
volumes, impeding deliveries and rescue operations [544][585]. Ambulance and civil-defence teams face persistent difficulties
reaching the wounded and recovering bodies in heavily affected Gaza areas, increasing civilian mortality and limiting response
capacity [579][555][560]. Attacks on health care and emergency responders and the increased use of explosive weapons and armed
drones across the theatre have further compounded civilian harm and impeded aid delivery, as documented by UN, AOAV and
Insecurity Insight monitoring [513][506][489]. 
The UN verified multiple incidents of conflict-related sexual violence against Palestinians in 2025, involving 14 men, 7 women, 9 boys
and 1 girl, raising acute protection and accountability concerns [507][548][521].
Conflict Stakeholders
Israel is the primary state actor conducting military operations in Gaza and the wider theatre. The UN Secretary-General's 2025 report
added Israeli armed and security forces, including the Israeli Prison Service, to the list of parties credibly suspected of conflict-related
sexual violence, citing verified incidents against Palestinians detained in Israel and the occupied Palestinian territory; cases date back
to 2023 and continued denial of access to detention centres constrained comprehensive verification [521][558][535]. 
Israel responded by freezing relations with UN Secretary-General António Guterres, with Ambassador Danny Danon announcing a
suspension of cooperation until the end of Guterres's term on 31 December 2026 [592][595].
Hamas remains the principal non-state armed actor in Gaza with significant territorial control, and Israel continues targeted
operations against Hamas leaders, sustaining a security-first dynamic that shapes reconstruction, governance arrangements and
political conditions in the territory [546][517][484]. 
Hamas has publicly accused UN Special Coordinator Nickolay Mladenov of incitement and demanded adherence to the ceasefire
plan, while continuing to blame Israeli violations for delays in implementation [566][488].
On the diplomatic and multilateral front, UN Special Coordinator Mladenov presented a 15-point road map to the Security Council in
May 2026, and the Peace Council report (S/2026/418) explicitly linked reconstruction, Israeli withdrawal and the deployment of an
International Stabilization Force to documented disarmament of Hamas, making Hamas disarmament a central obstacle to progress
[484]. 
Many states have conditioned troop pledges or limited contributions to the stabilization force on a deal for Hamas to disarm, leaving
pledges stalled and some commitments limited to humanitarian components only [517]. This linkage risks shifting reconstruction and
governance toward externally supervised "stability management," privileging security requirements over Palestinian institutional
authority and potentially producing new local actors aligned with international funders [484]. 
Regional mediators — notably Egypt, Qatar and Turkey — have played an active role in arranging negotiating rounds, while the US and
Pakistan have also participated in broader mediation efforts [488][569][596].
11
Peace Negotiations
Negotiations over Gaza's ceasefire and post-conflict transition remain active but fragile. The UN Peace Council submitted report
S/2026/418 on 15 May 2026, and Nickolay Mladenov briefed the Security Council with a 15-point roadmap linking documented Hamas
disarmament, a security transition, deployment of an International Stabilization Force and reconstruction [484]. 
Mladenov and the Peace Board have stated that the international stabilization force cannot begin operations until a second phase is
agreed in which Hamas undertakes documented disarmament and Israeli forces begin withdrawal [481][484].
Regional mediators Egypt, Qatar and Turkey have been arranging negotiating rounds. Egypt invited a senior Hamas negotiating
delegation led by Khalil al-Hayya for urgent talks in late May 2026 to try to salvage the ceasefire, after Hamas postponed a mid-May
trip to Cairo following the assassination of a senior Izz al-Din al-Qassam commander [496][566][488]. 
Ceasefire adherence on the ground remains poor, with repeated violations and reported Israeli expansion of control in Gaza — from
approximately 53% under ceasefire mapping toward an estimated 60–70% — undermining prospects for transition and reconstruction
[496][481][471]. Broader efforts are further constrained by mutual distrust and linkage to wider regional negotiations, including US–Iran
talks, limiting immediate political traction [488][569][596].
Conflict Outlook
The near-term outlook is marked by significant escalation risks and limited prospects for durable de-escalation. In late May 2026,
Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu announced plans to expand Israeli-controlled areas in Gaza from the ceasefire-mapped 53%
toward an initial 70%, following earlier expansions to roughly 64% of the enclave [552][545]. 
Hamas described the declaration as a dangerous escalation and warned it risks the forced displacement of Gaza's more than two
million residents [552][545]. 
Any further reduction in civilian-accessible territory would likely trigger significant additional displacement and further exacerbate the
humanitarian crisis, as essential services have already collapsed and critical aid shortfalls persist [470][484][545][552].
Concurrent Israeli operations in southern Lebanon and public threats from hard-line Israeli figures increase the likelihood of expansion
on adjacent fronts, raising the risk that Hezbollah-related exchanges will further destabilize the Gaza theatre [483][504][518]. 
The wider US–Iran crisis and attacks around the Strait of Hormuz divert diplomatic attention and raise regional escalation risks that
complicate prospects for a Gaza settlement and durable calm [470][501][500].
On the political track, Phase II steps remain fragile. International proposals that link reconstruction and Israeli withdrawal to
documented Palestinian disarmament — as embodied in the Peace Council/Mladenov roadmap — make rapid, unconditional
de-escalation less likely absent enforceable guarantees and credible monitoring [484]. 
Mediators, including Egypt, Qatar, and Peace Council envoys, continue to facilitate negotiations; however, multiple rounds of talks have
been repeatedly postponed due to targeted killings and renewed military operations, while deep-seated mutual distrust remains a
significant barrier to meaningful progress [488][566][484].
Displacement
Displacement Overview
UN estimates indicate around 90% of Gaza's more than 2 million residents have been displaced, equivalent to roughly 1.9–2.1 million
people, with approximately 1.7 million living in roughly 1,600 displacement sites across the Strip [667][649][652][673]. 
Humanitarian actors describe this displacement as protracted since the 2023 outbreak of hostilities — UNRWA counted days 955–960
as of 20–25 May 2026 — with repeated waves of displacement exacerbating shelter overcrowding and straining services
[633][624][603]. 
UNRWA reported renewed escalation with multiple evacuation and shelter-in-place orders during 20–25 May 2026, and humanitarian
partners recorded the displacement of over 150 families from eastern Khan Younis and eastern Gaza City between 16–17 May 2026, 40
of whom were identified as requiring emergency assistance after fleeing with only what they could carry; an 18 May airstrike in Jabalya
Camp reportedly damaged tents belonging to 35 families and displaced additional households [633][624][612]. 
Displaced populations are increasingly concentrated into shrinking habitable areas — reports indicate civilians confined to roughly
30–35% or less of the Strip — with displacement sites facing severe waste, sanitation, and rodent infestations that heighten
public-health risks, especially for children [603][652][649][606].
In the West Bank, the revival of long-standing demolition orders and a planned 1 June 2026 tender for 3,400 settlement units in the E1
area place 18 Bedouin and herding communities — about 4,000 people — at imminent risk of forced displacement [647][624].
Escalating settler violence, averaging six attacks per day in 2026, has displaced thousands since January 2025 and led to the
near-emptying of dozens of rural and herding communities [647]. Israeli officials' public discussion of "voluntary migration" has been
met by rights groups and legal experts who warn that such measures and the conditions prompting departure risk amounting to
forcible transfer under international law, while regional mediators — notably Egypt — have publicly cautioned against unilateral steps
that would lead to population displacement [614][641][660][652]. 
Prospects for returns or durable solutions remain limited as reconstruction and resettlement plans are stalled, and humanitarian
actors warn that temporary shelter footprints risk becoming permanent, complicating future recovery and repair of housing
[634][636].
12
Living Conditions of the Displaced Population
Most families in Gaza remain displaced, sheltering in overcrowded tents, schools, or damaged structures across temporary and
informal sites [624][673]. Accumulating solid waste, pest and rodent infestations, and large numbers of people living in flood-prone
displacement sites — estimated at around 800,000 people — are compounding public-health risks and straining sanitation services
[630][600][633][623]. 
UNRWA and health partners have reported rising ectoparasitic infections (scabies), rodent bites, chickenpox, and acute watery
diarrhoea, and nutrition partners recorded a 21% increase in malnutrition admissions between January–April 2026 compared with the
same period in 2025 [633]. OCHA and WHO warned that hospitals are largely non-functional or under extreme strain, with restrictions
on fuel, medicines, and movement impeding medical care and humanitarian operations [624][633].
On water and sanitation, UNICEF reported that between 1 January and 14 May 2026, some 1,000,000 people were supported to access
water, about 508,852 people accessed sanitation services, and over 603,300 people received critical WASH supplies [600]. As of 29
May, drinking-water distributions reached up to 1.5 million people depending on access, yet 82% of families remain water insecure and
up to 70% cannot collect the minimum 6 litres per person per day; desalination output fell from 20,000 to 16,000 m³/day due to
shortages of chemicals and spare parts [606]. The Al-Mansoura filling point remains inaccessible following a 17 April incident that
killed two UNICEF-contracted truck drivers, forcing partners to truck water from desalination plants at an additional cost of about USD
40,000 per day to replace some 2 million litres previously collected at that point, affecting approximately 285,000 people [606][611].
Roughly 1.2 million people are reported to have lost their homes, and health, water, sanitation, and waste-management infrastructure
have been heavily damaged [634][624]. Seasonal storms damaged shelters of more than 3,000 displaced people, and heatwaves
further undermine habitability [606][623]. Child protection partners provided mental health and psychosocial support (MHPSS) to over
4,000 children and approximately 1,500 caregivers between 11–17 May 2026, with weekly averages of about 1,600 children and 670
caregivers continuing to receive psychosocial services [612][624]. 
In the West Bank, Shelter Cluster partners reached over 9,300 households (around 40,300 people) with shelter assistance between 1
January and 30 April 2026, even as demolitions and demolition orders — particularly threats to Bedouin communities such as Khan
al-Ahmar — heighten the risk of forcible displacement [624][612].
Drivers of Displacement
Ongoing hostilities — including repeated strikes, tank movements, and shelling — continue to drive new and repeated displacement in
Gaza [624]. Mass destruction of housing and essential infrastructure has left many without safe alternatives, with roughly 1.2 million
people reported to have lost their homes and health, water, sanitation, and waste-management systems heavily damaged
[634][624]. 
Worsening environmental and climate shocks — accumulated solid waste, rodent and insect infestations, heatwaves, and seasonal
storms that damaged shelters of more than 3,000 displaced people, with around 800,000 people living in flood-prone areas — are
further undermining habitability and increasing public-health risks [606][623]. 
Water system collapse also contributes to displacement pressure: desalination output fell from 20,000 m³/day in March to 16,000
m³/day, and the suspension of the Al-Mansoura filling point since 17 April — following the killing of two UNICEF-contracted truck drivers
— forced alternative trucking at an additional cost of about USD 40,000 per day, constraining daily access for approximately 285,000
people [606].
In the West Bank, demolition orders and settlement expansion plans — notably in the E1 area — place Bedouin and herding
communities at acute risk of forcible displacement, with some 18 communities (about 4,000 people) directly affected and roughly 550
structures documented as demolished since 2009 [624]. Since the start of 2026, roughly 71% of approximately 400 structures
demolished in Area C were agricultural, livelihood-related, or WASH structures, reflecting a pattern that systematically erodes
livelihoods and habitability [647]. 
Escalating settler violence — with OCHA documenting some 870 attacks across more than 220 West Bank communities so far in 2026
(approximately six per day) — and conflict-related damage have driven growing shelter needs [624][647]. The Israeli authorities'
announcement of a 1 June 2026 tender to construct 3,400 new settlement units further fragments territory and risks forced
displacement [647].
The UN has documented conflict-related sexual violence and a systemic lack of accountability, which exacerbates protection risks for
detainees, returnees, and displaced women and girls; Palestinian authorities and rights actors have called for accountability and
access to justice for survivors [678][656].
Integration
Access to basic services and livelihoods remains severely constrained for displaced populations in Gaza and the West Bank. Protection
partners delivered services to almost 11,000 people between 11–17 May 2026, including MHPSS to 8,259 people, legal aid to 219, referrals
to 232, support to 341 persons with disabilities, and relief to 1,265 people; since the reopening of Rafah, partners have assisted 2,429
returnees, with 885 currently receiving follow-up protection services [624]. 
Child protection partners provide weekly MHPSS to about 1,600 children (including approximately 80 children with disabilities) and
more than 670 caregivers, with weekly awareness sessions reaching approximately 380 children and 100 caregivers; over a recent
week, cash and in-kind aid reached approximately 60 children and 12 caregivers, and 35 children received case management
[612][624].
13
In the West Bank, movement restrictions, raids, and administrative measures are severely impeding access to education, work, and
services, undermining integration prospects. In Hebron's H2 area, students faced repeated delays at the As-Salaymeh checkpoint, with
103 students unable to reach school on 11 May 2026 and similar disruptions recorded on 14 and 18 May [612][624]. The 17 May closure of
Burin village further disrupted access to work, education, and services [624]. 
Escalating settler violence and demolitions are eroding social cohesion and long-term settlement prospects: since January 2025,
violence has displaced thousands and led to the near-emptying of dozens of rural and herding communities, while threats to Khan
al-Ahmar and some 18 Bedouin and herding communities (around 4,000 people) remain acute [624][647][612]. 
The planned 1 June 2026 tender for 3,400 new settlement units in the E1 area is assessed as likely to further fragment territory and
constrain durable integration for affected communities [647].
Humanitarian Access
Humanitarian Access Overview
As of 25 May 2026, OCHA reported that humanitarian operations are concentrated across the Gaza Strip and the West Bank (including
East Jerusalem), with partners delivering shelter, protection, WASH, pest-control and food assistance amid widespread displacement
and constrained services [705]. Key operational actors include OCHA, UNDP, UNICEF, WFP, the Logistics Cluster, Shelter Cluster, Site
Management Cluster and protection partners, alongside local organizations and contested or emergent bodies such as the Gaza
Humanitarian Foundation [705][687][689].
The Site Management Cluster reported about 1,600 displacement sites hosting roughly 1.7 million people (354,480 households), nearly
88% in makeshift sites (data collected 3 Feb–10 May) [705][699]. More than two million Gaza residents remain displaced and
concentrated in overcrowded tents and shelters, increasing pressure on the remaining accessible areas [745][738]. UNDP led a
pest-control response launched on 17 May targeting over 1,700 locations, delivering 3 tonnes of rodenticides and 3,000 litres of
pesticides [705][699]. UNICEF reported reaching over 603,300 people with critical WASH supplies, with sector figures showing 1,000,000
people accessing water and 508,852 accessing sanitation; UNICEF's 2026 appeal (US$707.8 million) was 36% funded at the time of
reporting [687].
Humanitarian delivery is heavily dependent on land corridors, with Kerem Shalom and Zikim remaining the primary crossings for goods
and convoys. Logistics Cluster data showed variable offloading rates—only about half of trucks from Egypt could offload at Kerem
Shalom in the first 18 days of May, with recorded offloading rates of 81% between 11–17 May [705][699]. Donor and bilateral modalities,
including ambulance and medical evacuations and a floating hospital operating from Al Arish, augment land-based deliveries but
cannot offset persistent corridor, regulatory and security constraints [727][705][699]. Maritime routes also remain blocked following
the interception of the Global Sumud Flotilla and other solidarity fleets by Israeli naval forces in late April–mid May 2026 [702][728].
OCHA and partners report administrative impediments imposed by Israeli authorities—including movement coordination
requirements for humanitarian teams and restrictions on shelter items and the approval of core WASH and medical imports—which
humanitarian actors say must be lifted to scale responses [705][687]. Operational bottlenecks cited by partners include chronic fuel
shortages limiting hospitals, water pumping and generators, rising transport costs, and reduced stocks of essential household and
shelter items, undermining sustained operations [691][705].
Barriers to Humanitarian Access
Humanitarian access in Gaza remained severely constrained by ongoing hostilities, Israeli Defence Forces operations, evacuation
orders, and damage to roads, crossings, hospitals and other civilian infrastructure, repeatedly disrupting delivery of food, fuel,
medicines and medical supplies [688][698]. Security constraints remain acute: Israeli strikes, shelling and other hostilities repeatedly
damage residential areas and humanitarian infrastructure, with partners recording displacement of more than 150 families between
16–17 May [705][701]. Humanitarian facilities and assets were themselves affected in early May 2026, with reports of a UN warehouse
struck by an Israeli airstrike, an MSF team area impacted by tank fire injuring civilians, and an airstrike reported near multiple
humanitarian facilities in Deir al-Balah, all undermining safe operations and staff security [702].
Administrative impediments from Israeli authorities prevented entry of water testing equipment and liquid chlorine supplies to
hospitals and primary health-care centres, constraining infection prevention and WASH interventions [705]. UNICEF called on
authorities to facilitate safe, sustained and predictable humanitarian access and to enable entry and approval of core
items—including pumps, pipes, fuel and other essential supplies—required to stabilise water and sanitation services [687].
Maritime attempts to deliver aid were intercepted at sea, with the Global Sumud Flotilla and other solidarity fleets seized or stopped by
Israeli naval forces in late April–mid May 2026, with participants reporting mistreatment in detention, further complicating sea-borne
humanitarian routes [702][728].
In the West Bank, OCHA documented escalating access constraints from settler violence and demolitions, noting more than 50 settler
attacks in one week and an average of six attacks per day in 2026, restricting movement and access to services [705]. Renewed
concerns emerged that rapid implementation of long-standing demolition orders could forcibly displace communities; OCHA has
documented roughly 550 structures demolished in affected communities since 2009 [705].
Humanitarian Access Gaps
Humanitarian access is severely constrained across Gaza, particularly where Israeli-controlled and restricted zones have expanded
beyond the October ceasefire line—from 53% toward approximately 64%, with a stated plan to reach an initial 70%—shrinking space for
civilians and aid delivery [738][742][751]. Humanitarians report that little aid reaches many civilians and that distribution is frequently
dangerous and chaotic, undermining the effectiveness of current access strategies [738][708][756]. Operational constraints cited by
14
logistics actors include designated restricted zones on maps shared with aid groups, blocked crossings and constrained regional
supply routes that complicate deliveries and lengthen lead times [742][761][719].
Health-care access is critically degraded, with no fully functioning hospital across Gaza, rising infectious diseases, and attacks and
other violence against health services and workers compounded by funding cuts that have dramatically reduced essential services
[756][698]. UNICEF reported alarming WASH and environmental-health risks, including growing solid waste at displacement sites,
limited or inaccessible disposal and landfill capacity, rodent and pest infestations, and constrained access to potable water as critical
filling points (including Al Mansoura) have become inaccessible [714][756].
Food insecurity is acute: the World Food Programme estimates approximately 1.6 million people (77% of the population) face high
levels of acute food insecurity, including over 100,000 children and 37,000 pregnant and nursing women [767]. Access to detained
Palestinians and independent monitoring is also constrained, with the UN reporting that the ICRC has been denied access to detention
facilities since 7 October 2023, limiting humanitarian oversight and protection interventions [726].
Information, Communication and AAP
Information Landscape
Humanitarian data collection in Gaza draws on a range of established systems. UNICEF reported that in 2026 an estimated 1,000,000
people accessed water, 508,852 accessed sanitation services, and 603,312 people were reached with critical WASH supplies, with its
Gaza hotline answering over 85,000 calls and using feedback on unmet hygiene needs and irregular water supply to adapt WASH
programmes [769]. OCHA's oPt situation reporting (25 May 2026) recorded that 54 partners provide approximately 24,000 cubic
metres of water per day to more than 2,000 locations, with about 74% of households in Gaza relying on such deliveries, and that
between 4–17 May partners distributed 15,343 hygiene kits, 2,448 dignity kits and 7,350 jerry cans, benefitting nearly 116,000 people
[775]. Protection monitoring combined qualitative methods and community engagement: eight focus-group discussions and 130
key-informant interviews across 16 neighbourhoods reached 1,155 people, informing protection reporting and referrals [801][775].
Between 11–17 May, partners provided MHPSS to over 4,000 children and approximately 1,500 caregivers, delivered child-protection
awareness activities for about 4,700 children and caregivers, provided individual case management to 56 newly identified high-risk
children and continued follow-up for more than 3,000 active child protection cases [801][775]. Partners also supported the medical
evacuation of 59 patients (including six children) to Egypt via Rafah, alongside 87 caregivers [775][801]. Mine-action reporting
indicates that since the October 2025 ceasefire there have been 109 recorded explosive-hazard accidents causing 265 injuries and 49
deaths, based on UNMAS and partners' assessments [801]. Shelter and distribution monitoring shows partners installed 115 emergency
shelters in Gaza and Khan Younis and the Rapid Joint Distribution Mechanism supported 55 households with emergency shelter and
essential household items [775][801]. The SHCC/Insecurity Insight incident-monitoring approach underlines strict data responsibility,
transparency and ethical handling of sensitive information to protect sources and inform collective understanding [774]. Operational
reporting routinely identifies gaps that constrain both response and information systems — notably shortages of spare parts, fuel,
transport and predictable funding — which partners flag as impeding sustained monitoring and programme delivery [775][801].
Communication Channels
Humanitarian actors and affected people rely primarily on phone-based hotlines, VHF radio networks, and face-to-face interactions
including focus groups, key-informant interviews and community outreach at displacement sites and shelters [769][775]. UNICEF
operates a hotline that answered over 85,000 calls and uses confidential feedback and community participation to adapt WASH and
other programmes [769]. Connectivity in Gaza remains severely constrained, limiting humanitarian coordination; the VHF network is
only partially functional due to security constraints and limited technical capacity, and the Emergency Telecommunications Cluster
(ETC) worked with UNDSS and PRCS/ICRC on VHF recovery and a Communications Plan with technical inputs expected by 31 May 2026
[775][801]. Phone interviews and in-person visits (data collected between 3 February and 10 May) are used for displacement-site
population estimates, but repeated internet restrictions and overall limited connectivity hamper regular, reliable digital reporting and
information flows [775][774].
Barriers to Information
Movement and access restrictions — including fragmentation that prevents travel between the Gaza Strip and the West Bank, military
closed zones, and requirements for humanitarian teams to coordinate movements with Israeli authorities — impede both information
flow and aid delivery; only half of aid trucks from Egypt could offload at Kerem Shalom in the first 18 days of May, further constraining
supplies and equipment needed for communications and services [775]. Explosive hazards and access insecurity have restricted safe
assessments and monitoring, with 109 accidents recorded since the October 2025 ceasefire causing 265 injuries and 49 deaths,
undermining safe access for technical recovery and debris removal [801][775]. Operationally, the WASH Cluster warned that severe
shortages of engine oil (an estimated ~7,000 litres required monthly) and lack of spare parts are disrupting water, sanitation and
wastewater services and emergency repairs [769][801]. Monitoring and accountability face political and institutional barriers: the
United Nations verified 31 cases of sexual abuse in Israeli detention settings in 2025 and reported significant obstacles to UN monitoring
in Gaza, including denials of International Red Cross access and lack of information from Israeli authorities demonstrating compliance
with preventive measures [858][835]. Media and narrative barriers have also been noted, with analysts arguing that structural biases
in Western media marginalize Palestinian testimonies and limit dissemination of local perspectives [818].
Misinformation and Rumors
Information flows in Gaza are constrained by repeated internet restrictions and the expulsion of some aid actors, which likely result in
under-reporting and gaps in verified information and incident documentation [774]. Contested narratives about humanitarian
delivery — illustrated by criticism of new aid structures such as the Gaza Humanitarian Foundation — have increased public scepticism
over the neutrality, access and accountability of aid distribution [777]. Rights groups document large numbers of missing children and
15
allege enforced concealments near aid distribution points, fuelling fear and uncertainty among relatives and civil society actors
seeking reliable information [789]. Incidents affecting aid operations — for example the interception of a Gaza-bound flotilla and
strikes reported near medical teams — generate competing media accounts that complicate independent verification of events and
humanitarian access reports [814]. To strengthen information integrity and trust, UNICEF emphasises accessible information,
community participation and confidential feedback mechanisms, alongside ongoing protection monitoring and community
consultations by protection partners conducted in mid-May [769][801][775].
Accountability Needs and Transparency
Humanitarian actors and mediators have repeatedly called for transparent, verifiable monitoring of aid flows — including publishing
truck-entry figures, protecting convoys and guaranteeing fuel — after plans for 600 daily truck deliveries were not met and only about
half of trucks from Egypt were offloaded in the first 18 days of May, undermining civilians' ability to judge implementation of ceasefire
commitments [798][776]. OCHA and partners report persistent access constraints that impede accountability and monitoring: aid
workers face significant movement restrictions requiring coordination with Israeli authorities, and the VHF communications network
remains only partially functional, limiting field coordination and feedback capacity [775][801]. The UN has documented a systemic lack
of accountability for conflict-related sexual violence against Palestinians, verifying multiple cases in 2025 (including 14 men, 7 women,
9 boys and 1 girl) and highlighting a case where charges against five soldiers were dropped in March 2026, while urging Israel to allow
UN access and conduct independent investigations — gaps that erode trust in remedial mechanisms and deter reporting by victims
[768][835][772]. Denial of access to detention facilities for UN monitors and reports that detainees face threats discouraging
complaints further weaken accountability and independent oversight [768][772]. Local engagement and accountability efforts exist at
small scale — including legal awareness sessions for women and community-level protection and child-protection case
management — but partners report that these services, feedback mechanisms and case-management capacities remain
underfunded and insufficient to meet needs across Gaza and the West Bank [836][801].
Legal and Policy
Legal and Policy Framework
UN Security Council Resolution 2803 (adopted 2025), together with the Peace Council's first report (S/2026/418) and Nickolay
Mladenov's briefing of 21 May 2026, frames Gaza reconstruction as conditional on security arrangements and Palestinian Authority
reform. Critics argue this configuration risks shifting Gaza toward an international trusteeship model rather than restoring Palestinian
sovereignty [874]. Palestinian representatives at the UN, including Ambassador Riyad Mansour, have explicitly rejected approaches
that separate reconstruction from political rights and called for balanced application of international law to all parties [874].
Palestinian institutions and international interlocutors are also calling for use and preservation of international legal
instruments—including the ICJ advisory opinion of July 2024 and international prosecution mechanisms—to secure accountability and
prevent reconstruction frameworks from conditioning Palestinian self-determination on performance measures [874][952].
The UN Special Representative on Sexual Violence in Conflict, Pramila Patten, presented the Secretary-General's 17th annual report in
May 2026, listing Israeli forces as suspected of patterns of conflict-related sexual violence, verifying 31 cases (13 in 2025; 18 dating to
2023–24), noting denial of UN and ICRC access to detention facilities, and recording that sexual-violence charges in the Sde Teiman
case were dropped in March 2026, raising significant accountability concerns [893][922][912].
Israeli legislative and administrative proposals cited in reporting—notably a draft law to transfer management of West Bank heritage
sites to an Israeli "Judaea and Samaria Heritage Authority" and offshore gas licensing plans—are described as measures that would
alter the legal and administrative status of occupied territory and risk de facto annexation under international law [894][930]. OCHA's
25 May 2026 humanitarian situation reporting documents severe impediments to humanitarian access, restricted crossings, and
widespread displacement and demolitions across the West Bank and Gaza that affect implementation of legal protections and
delivery of aid [879]. At the civil-society level, Palestinian organizations continue to provide legal protection and awareness
services—for example, a SPEED legal-awareness session for women in Ein Arik on 12 May 2026—to help individuals navigate domestic
remedies and protection mechanisms amid weakening institutional safeguards [910].
Humanitarian Impact of Legislation
Humanitarian access across the Occupied Palestinian Territory remained heavily constrained in late May 2026 by Israeli movement
restrictions, military raids, checkpoints, and administrative barriers that disrupt aid deliveries and internal movement between Gaza
and the West Bank [881][879]. OCHA documented more than 40 raids across the West Bank during the reporting period (mid–late May
2026), involving house searches, temporary home evacuations, and prolonged closures that disrupted education, livelihoods, and
access to services [881][879]. Only half of aid trucks from Egypt were able to offload at the Israeli-controlled Kerem Shalom crossing in
the first 18 days of May 2026, and Kerem Shalom remained the only operational cargo crossing while Zikim was closed, constraining
the volume and predictability of food, fuel, and medical imports [881][936]. Israeli policy measures since 2025–January 2026 have
included bans and licence revocations affecting UNRWA and international NGOs, with authorities requiring detailed personal
information on Palestinian staff and revoking licences for MSF and dozens of other organisations after they refused to comply
[885][897]. Humanitarian partners further reported that Israeli authorities require coordination of humanitarian movements in some
areas, imposing bureaucratic impediments to shelter, WASH, and public-health responses including pest-control and fuel deliveries
[879][936].
These operational and administrative restrictions have contributed to a collapsing health response in Gaza: reporting in late May 2026
warned that 250 dialysis patients risk losing access, some 11,000 diabetic patients face insulin shortages, 110 people with haemophilia
lack treatment, and roughly 76% of medical imaging equipment has been destroyed, undermining nondiscriminatory access to health
care [890][898]. Human Rights Watch, UN reporting, and health actors have documented widespread attacks on health care and aid
16
workers—including high numbers of aid-worker deaths since 2023—and have called for stronger legal protections, compliance with IHL,
and repeal of laws that criminalise health workers for providing impartial care [917][931][928][885].
In the West Bank, revival of long-standing demolition orders and the opening of tenders for 3,400 settlement units in the E1 area risk
imminent forced displacement of Bedouin communities including Khan al-Ahmar and further restrict rights to housing, land, and
access to services [879][933]. Administrative practices at checkpoints—such as demands for birth certificates or parental
