accompaniment that prevented students from reaching school in Hebron in mid-May 2026—illustrate legal and bureaucratic barriers
limiting freedom of movement and access to education and essential services [879].
International Agreements Impacting the Crisis
Resolution 2803 established a Board of Peace/Peace Council, an international stabilization force, and a periodic reporting mechanism,
but the Board faces a severe funding shortfall with roughly 1% of the approximately $17 billion pledged reported as transferred,
undermining its operational capacity [874][890]. Provisions in the October 2025 Gaza agreement envisaged a multilateral peace
operation, but plans for deployment and broader UN peacekeeping engagement remain stalled amid geopolitical divisions and
limited UN capacity [905].
The International Criminal Court issued arrest warrants in November 2024 against senior Israeli officials, creating an active judicial
accountability track [889]. The International Court of Justice and the UN General Assembly have also applied pressure—characterising
the occupation as unlawful and issuing a GA demand to end the occupation by September 2025—with commentators and some
Member States calling for measures such as arms embargoes and economic pressure [902]. UN Secretary-General reporting and UN
mechanisms have placed Israeli entities on the list of parties suspected of conflict-related sexual violence, a development explicitly
welcomed by the Palestinian Ministry of Women as requiring accountability [913][893]. The Government of Israel has publicly rejected
those UN findings, suspended substantive cooperation with the Secretary-General's office, and denied UN and ICRC access to some
detention facilities, restricting international investigative avenues [887][893].
Humanitarian and operational frameworks have been further constrained by an Israeli ban on UNRWA operations in areas under Israeli
jurisdiction and restrictive conditions on NGOs, while WHO and other UN bodies adopted monitoring and reporting mandates on health
in the occupied Palestinian territory [885][895].
Limitations of Current Policies
International policy signals risk turning reconstruction into conditional trusteeship that links Gaza recovery to political and security
reforms, transforming the right to self-determination into performance-conditioned arrangements—a trend flagged in analyses of
Resolution 2803 and recent Peace Council briefings in May 2026 [874]. Agreements and international mechanisms lack effective
implementation and monitoring: mediators and the Peace Council have been criticised for failing to ensure sustained border
openings, measure and publish truck entry data, and protect convoys, rendering formal commitments ineffective for civilians on the
ground [880][874]. Overall, international resolutions and policy statements frequently remain disconnected from practice, normalising
monitoring and reporting without delivering enforceable measures that change access, protection, or accountability outcomes for
Palestinians [895][882].
Humanitarian access remains chronically insufficient: the planned 600 trucks-per-day protocol has not been met, and critical service
delivery is failing as a result [880][881]. WASH operations face severe fuel shortages (the WASH Cluster estimates approximately 7,000
litres per month needed), with infrastructure shutdowns—including the Sheikh Radwan lagoon closed on 11 May—and inaccessible
sanitary landfills causing pest outbreaks [879][890]. Severe funding shortfalls and donor austerity have reduced lifesaving operations,
with humanitarian kitchens cutting meal provision by around 80% (from approximately 1,000,000 to approximately 200,000 daily
meals), exacerbating dependence on inconsistent cross-border access and undermining response adaptability [886].
Administrative and security conditions imposed by Israeli authorities—including requirements to coordinate movements, restrictive
vetting or data demands for staff, bans or threats to NGOs, and measures limiting UNRWA operations—have further impeded
humanitarian actors and curtailed operational space [879][885][889]. Accountability and enforcement mechanisms remain weak: UN
reporting documents credible allegations of conflict-related sexual violence and a systemic lack of investigations or access to
detention sites, while international monitoring and accountability for attacks on health care remain limited despite extensive
documentation of incidents in 2025 [922][893][917][935].
17
Sectoral Analysis
Food Security and Livelihoods
Access to Food and Market Functionality
Market supplies in Gaza remain constrained, with commercial and humanitarian market assessments reporting elevated but generally
stable prices while fresh products show high volatility, limiting normal market recovery [1010]. Crossing restrictions and reduced
commercial imports are limiting availability and recovery, and the Cash Working Group (CWG) has advocated for increased
commercial truck entries, reduced fees on essential commodities, and a better import balance to stabilise markets [1010][969]. The
humanitarian protocol target of 600 trucks per day has not been met in practice, with actual volumes reported below one-third
(approximately 38%) of pre-war levels, constraining market resupply and staple availability [969]. Purchasing power and liquidity have
collapsed amid massive market and labour-market losses, driving sharp price surges, with reports that one in five families survives on
a single small meal per day and caregivers reducing rations for children [962][964]. A concurrent cash-flow crisis — scarcity of cash,
banking disruptions, and exorbitant commissions — has prevented many people and traders from accessing funds, compounding
food insecurity and daily survival needs [962]. Market disruption has pushed prices to unaffordable levels, with reports of sheep priced
above 15,000 ILS and meat at approximately USD 80–115/kg, further eroding purchasing power [979][962].
Coping Mechanisms
Households in Gaza are reducing food consumption and meal frequency, with many adults — particularly mothers — skipping or
cutting their own meals to prioritise children, and field reports indicating one in five families surviving on only one snack per day [964].
Families have resorted to selling livestock early or slaughtering animals for food, and repeated displacement has depleted savings
and productive assets while stripping households of cooking tools and basic possessions [979][962][974]. Primitive indoor cooking
resulting from displacement has caused fires, increasing protection and psychosocial risks, and prompting scaled-up child protection
and cash interventions to reduce exposure to negative coping mechanisms [1015][974][973]. Reductions in large-scale food
assistance — including World Central Kitchen cutting daily cooked meals by approximately 80% (from 1,000,000 to 200,000) and
dismissing over 500 local staff — have increased the risk of famine for many displaced households who had been dependent on such
assistance for basic meals [964][1015]. The UN 2026 flash appeal sought USD 4 billion for 3 million Palestinians but had received only
roughly 12% (~USD 490 million), triggering protests in Deir el-Balah and further constraining humanitarian food delivery [964]. Since the
Rafah reopening, partners have assisted return movements — with 2,429 returnees supported and 885 receiving follow-up protection
services — reflecting mobility linked to the crisis and relief access dynamics [1010].
Livelihood Disruption
Large-scale destruction of markets, businesses, and factories has caused thousands of business closures and mass unemployment,
sharply depleting savings and reducing household purchasing power across Gaza and the West Bank [962]. Since 2021, the Palestinian
public sector has faced a salary crisis; by mid-2025, public-sector workers had accumulated billions in unpaid wages, undermining a
key source of stable employment and prompting avoidance of government jobs [992]. The collapse in local labour markets is also
affecting future prospects: graduates face a contracted job market, rising unemployment, and increasing out-migration as many seek
work abroad or in informal, lower-paid jobs [992]. In the West Bank, movement restrictions, raids, and targeted demolitions have
further disrupted livelihoods in Area C: OCHA documented more than 40 raids during the reporting period and reports that, since the
start of 2026, approximately 71% of roughly 400 structures demolished in Area C for lacking Israeli permits were agricultural,
livelihood-related, or water and sanitation structures — including one incident in which 18 of 19 structures demolished were
livelihood-related — causing significant financial losses to affected households [1010].
Impact on Agricultural Activities and Production
The agricultural sector in Gaza has been devastated. An FAO report from October 2024 found approximately 67.6% of agricultural land
damaged, 70% of poultry farms destroyed, and over 71% of gardens and fruit trees affected, while local authorities report more than
80% of agricultural land destroyed and direct losses across 15 vital sectors exceeding USD 33 billion [979]. The Palestinian Ministry of
Agriculture estimates total agricultural sector losses exceeding USD 4 billion [979]. FAO and satellite analysis further indicate that less
than 5% of Gaza's agricultural land remains usable, and hundreds of greenhouses in Rafah and Khan Younis — once supplying over
40% of the Strip's fresh vegetables — have been razed and topsoil confiscated [988]. Livestock numbers have collapsed: sheep and
goats declined from approximately 60,000 to roughly 3,000, dairy cattle fell from around 3,000 to just two or three animals, and
pre-war calf imports of roughly 3,000 per month have effectively ceased [979]. Primary drivers include direct shelling of barns and
farms, loss of feed and water, shortages of veterinary medicines, forced slaughter in the early months of the war, and continuing
restrictions on live-animal imports that have undermined meat and dairy production [979]. Damage to markets, slaughterhouses, and
transport infrastructure has reduced market functionality and eliminated livelihoods for many rural and peri-urban producers,
constraining future food production and rural income recovery [979][1010]. Ongoing displacement, repeated access restrictions, and
insecurity have left farmers unable to reach or tend their fields, making large agricultural zones inaccessible or abandoned and further
disrupting planting, harvesting, and local supply chains [967][988].
Food and Livelihood Needs and Current Response
Humanitarian partners provided general food assistance in May, with 122,000 households (440,000 people) receiving parcels
consisting of one 25-kg flour bag and 2.5 kg of high-energy biscuits, covering approximately 75% of minimum caloric needs with
additional calories supplied through other modalities [1010][973]. As of 13 May, partners were preparing and serving roughly 1 million
meals per day through 103 kitchens to almost 1,800 locations, though cooked-meal production has been scaled down due to funding
constraints while responders seek to diversify assistance toward more cash and livelihood support [973][1010]. Bread production is
supported through subsidized bakeries, community ovens, and flour distributions, producing at least 300 metric tons of bread daily —
18
approximately 36% of the Strip's estimated bread needs — with 28 subsidized bakeries producing roughly 130,000 two-kilogram
bundles per day (approximately 80% sold at a subsidized price of 3 NIS through 168 contracted retailers and approximately 20%
distributed free to shelters) [973][1010]. UNOPS delivered and partners distributed just over 2.1 million litres of diesel into Gaza between
7–20 May to support humanitarian operations and sustain fuel-dependent food and water services [1010]. Coverage gaps remain
large: approximately 1.6 million people in Gaza (roughly 77% of the population) face high levels of acute food insecurity, and OCHA has
warned that funding shortfalls are severely hampering basic relief operations, with the UN 2026 flash appeal for 3 million Palestinians
having received only approximately 12% (~USD 490 million) at reporting [964][1051]. Scaling up local food production — including home
gardening with seedlings, technical advice, seed-saving, and compost — and livelihoods remains constrained by lack of inputs,
technical support, and restricted access; partners identify timely, unrestricted entry of agricultural inputs through commercial and
humanitarian channels and support for local importers as critical gaps to restart sustainable livelihoods at scale [1010][973].
Education
Learning Environment and Facility Conditions
Over 97% of schools in Gaza have been damaged or destroyed, leaving some 658,000 children without formal learning for more than
two years [1070][1054]. Universities including the Islamic University of Gaza, AlAzhar, and AlIsraa have been razed, eliminating tertiary
education capacity for tens of thousands of students [1070]. Many schools that remain standing have been repurposed as
overcrowded shelters, with entire classrooms hosting dozens of displaced people, undermining privacy, routine, and the feasibility of
inschool learning [1053][1055][1052][1056][1070]. Schools and UNRWA installations have also been directly affected by evacuation and
shelterinplace orders, further constraining the use of educational facilities [1055]. Airstrikes and nearby strikes have damaged or
threatened school and humanitarian facilities, including a strike near five humanitarian facilities in Deir alBalah and an 18 May strike
that damaged 35 families' tents in Jabalya Camp, further undermining safe learning environments [1069][1053]. In the West Bank,
checkpoint restrictions and delays have disrupted students' physical access to schooling, with repeated incidents at As Salaymeh
checkpoint where approximately 100 students faced demands for birth certificates or parental accompaniment, and 103 students were
unable to reach school on 11 May, with similar incidents on 14 and 18 May [1052][1053]. Prolonged Israeli raids and temporary village
closures also disrupted learning environments—a 17 May raid on Burin closed all village entrances for nearly 19 hours, forcing some
schools to postpone exams and suspend classes [1053]. Settler actions have further blocked school access, including a barbedwire
fence that prevented more than 50 children in Umm AlKheir from entering their school for over two weeks and the reported demolition
of a school in the northern Jordan Valley [1060]. Longerterm data indicate that up to 20% of West Bank schools were closed during the
2023/24 academic year due to military operations or settler violence, producing learning losses of up to three months for affected
children [1060]. Access constraints, insecurity, and administrative impediments continue to hinder the delivery of education services
and partner activities across the occupied Palestinian territory [1052][1053].
Access to Education and Barriers
Beyond physical destruction of facilities, a range of compounding barriers is preventing children from accessing education. Acute
WASH failures, rodent infestations, and outbreaks of skin and diarrhoeal diseases in displacement sites are intensifying barriers to
attendance and safe learning in Gaza, with partners reporting increases in ectoparasitic infections, rodent bites, and acute watery
diarrhoea in May 2026 [1055][1059][1064]. Fuel shortages and rising transportation costs are further constraining schooling and
education operations by limiting mobility for students, families, and staff, and reducing the capacity of education and protection
partners to maintain outreach and services [1052]. Displacement, economic strain, and protection concerns have also driven social
barriers to girls' education: reports indicate a rise in child marriage amid the crisis, with Supreme Shariah Court data showing that
20.6% of recorded marriages in 2024–2025 involved girls under 18, trends that risk increased school dropout for girls [1057][1058].
Education Needs and Current Response
In response to the near-total collapse of Gaza's education system, UNRWA opened a new Temporary Learning Space (TLS) on 20 May
2026 at a displacement site south of Khan Younis; within a week, 1,600 children had registered, with the TLS established in collaboration
with education cluster partners [1055]. Between 11–17 May, partners provided Mental Health and Psychosocial Support (MHPSS) to over
4,000 children and approximately 1,500 caregivers [1053], with child protection partners reporting an average of approximately 1,600
children and more than 670 caregivers reached weekly through MHPSS and child-friendly activities [1052]. However, coordination and
funding remain insufficient to restore safe, continuous learning at scale amid ongoing displacement and infrastructure destruction
[1055][1053][1070]. Priority needs and gaps include large-scale rehabilitation of damaged schools and higher-education facilities,
expansion of Temporary Learning Spaces and non-formal learning where schools are unusable, recruitment and payment of trained
teachers, provision of learning materials, and integrated psychosocial support for children and caregivers. Partners report urgent
shortages of funding, fuel, transport, and operational supplies to sustain outreach, home visits, and child-friendly spaces
[1053][1052][1063].
Health
Availability and Access to Healthcare Services
Gaza's health system has effectively collapsed under the weight of sustained conflict. WHO has documented more than 900 attacks
on health facilities since October 2023, leaving approximately 84% of healthcare facilities damaged or destroyed, with only about half
of 36 hospitals partially functioning and none operational in northern Gaza [1123][1158]. Critical medical shortages include 250 people
on dialysis at risk of losing access to treatment, 11,000 diabetic patients facing insulin shortages, and 110 people with haemophilia
lacking essential care. Medical imaging capacity is heavily degraded, with approximately 76% of equipment destroyed, all nine MRI
units lost, and only five of 18 CT scanners functioning [1128].
19
AlAqsa Martyrs Hospital reported generator failures and severe spareparts shortages that have forced the suspension of more than
50% of services, threatening continuity of surgery, dialysis, ICU and laboratory functions for a population of more than half a million
people [1138].
Between 11–17 May, partners supported the medical evacuation of 59 patients (including six children) and 87 caregivers via Rafah to
Egypt, and provided clinical consultations across 181 reporting sites [1119][1118]. Infection prevention and control trainings were
completed for over 400 clinical staff across five major hospitals and more than 100 environmental cleaners and emergency medical
services personnel, though hospitals and primary health centres continue to report shortages of water-testing equipment and liquid
chlorine due to administrative impediments to imports and humanitarian access [1118][1119][1125]. Only half of aid trucks from Egypt
could offload at Israeli-controlled crossings in the first 18 days of May 2026, constraining delivery of medicines, fuel and medical
supplies [1128][1119].
WASH partners provide roughly 24,000 cubic metres of water per day to over 2,000 locations—deliveries that approximately 74% of
Gaza households rely upon—yet average household water availability remains at only approximately 4.5–6 litres per person per day,
exacerbating public health risks [1118][1119][1139]. In the West Bank, mobile clinics remain a primary lifeline for isolated communities;
Medical Aid for Palestinians' mobile teams have reached over 33,000 Palestinians across 22 communities since 2025, while settlement
expansion, checkpoints and settler violence continue to restrict movement to hospitals and clinics and deter patients and health
workers from travelling [1154][1168]. On 25 May, the Palestinian Prime Minister met with ICRC representatives to press for intensified field
hospitals, facilitated entry of medicines and medical supplies, and increased medical evacuations for wounded Palestinians requiring
treatment outside the Strip [1145].
Disease Outbreaks
Between 11–17 May, reportable diseases accounted for 21.4% of consultations across 181 reporting sites, with increasing trends in skin
diseases, acute watery diarrhoea and bloody diarrhoea, while acute respiratory infections declined [1119]. UNRWA medical surveillance
teams additionally reported rising ectoparasitic infections (including scabies), rodent bites and chickenpox among children and older
groups [1125]. WHO environmental surveillance for March–April 2026 returned all samples negative for poliovirus, marking 12
consecutive months without detection pending an OBRA desk review [1119].
Severe WASH-related risks are driving and amplifying disease transmission. UNICEF reported that 82% of families remain water
insecure, with up to 70% unable to collect the minimum 6 litres per person per day for drinking and cooking [1122][1139]. Seawater
desalination output fell from approximately 20,000 m³/day in March to 16,000 m³/day due to shortages of chemicals and spare parts,
and the Al Mansoura filling point has been inaccessible since a 17 April attack that killed two UNICEF-contracted drivers, necessitating
costly trucking of approximately 2 million litres per day at around USD 40,000/day [1122][1139]. Severe shortages of engine
oil—estimated at roughly 7,000 litres needed monthly—continue to disrupt critical water and wastewater operations and prompted the
shutdown of the Sheikh Radwan stormwater lagoon on 11 May, raising flooding and public health risks [1119][1146].
The UN and partners deployed a rodent control campaign—three tonnes of rodenticides and 3,000 litres of pesticides across
approximately 1,700 sites—but access to sanitary landfills and debris-removal equipment remains blocked, sustaining conditions for
pest infestation and disease transmission [1146][1119]. Shortages of water-testing equipment and liquid chlorine in hospitals and
primary health centres, linked to administrative impediments on imports, further weaken prevention and response capacity [1119].
Multiple UN agencies warn that damaged sanitation, overcrowding, disrupted waste management and constrained fuel and supply
access are collectively driving escalating risks of diarrhoeal, skin and rodent-associated diseases [1122][1118][1125].
Health System Challenges
Gaza's health system has effectively collapsed under repeated attacks on facilities, damage to infrastructure, fuel and supply
blockades, and mass displacement, producing widespread service failure and surge needs [1143][1123]. Approximately 84% of
healthcare facilities have been damaged or destroyed, with some sources citing approximately 1,800 facilities affected since October
2023, and WHO has recorded more than 900 attacks on health infrastructure since that time [1158]. Violence against healthcare in
2025 and ongoing hostilities have driven workforce losses, facility attacks and supply-chain disruption; local health actors report
cumulative high losses among health personnel since October 2023, further weakening service delivery capacity [1143][1149]. The
Safeguarding Health in Conflict Coalition documented widespread incidents affecting health services and health workers, contributing
to reduced functionality and staffing [1143].
Repeated damage to roads, crossings, warehouses and power infrastructure, combined with restrictions on fuel and imports, have
halted or degraded hospital operations, cold chains and pharmaceutical supplies [1117][1138]. Generator failures at facilities such as
AlAqsa Martyrs Hospital have pushed services into a "high-risk phase," with over 50% of hospital services suspended and the facility
serving more than half a million people [1138]. Only half of aid trucks from Egypt could offload at Israeli-controlled crossings in the first
18 days of May 2026, directly constraining the delivery of medicines, fuel and medical supplies needed to sustain services [1128][1119].
Water insecurity compounds health system pressure: UNICEF reported 82% of families remain water insecure, desalination output fell
from approximately 20,000 to 16,000 m³/day due to shortages of chemicals and spare parts, and hospitals and primary health centres
lack water-testing equipment and liquid chlorine due to administrative impediments on imports [1122][1119]. Sanitary landfills remain
inaccessible, undermining pest-control efforts and increasing public health risks in displacement sites [1128]. Reconstruction and
recovery financing remain inadequate, with multilateral mechanisms reporting severe funding shortfalls that undermine plans to
restore health infrastructure and services [1128][1133].
20
Mental Health and Psychosocial Support (MHPSS)
Community-based psychosocial support is the primary MHPSS modality in the occupied Palestinian territory, complemented by
recreational and structured support activities and parenting sessions [1119][1118]. On average each week, child protection partners
provide MHPSS to roughly 1,600 children (including about 80 children with disabilities) and more than 670 caregivers [1119][1118].
During the reporting period of 11–17 May 2026, protection partners provided MHPSS—including psychological first aid and individual and
group counselling—to 8,259 people, while humanitarian actors overall reported providing MHPSS and other protection services to more
than 10,000 people [1119][1118][1141]. Child protection partners provided MHPSS to over 4,000 children and approximately 1,500
caregivers, and conducted awareness and community-based protection activities reaching an additional approximately 4,700
children and caregivers [1119][1118]. Case management and follow-up during the same period included 56 newly identified high-risk
children receiving individual case management, continued follow-up for more than 3,000 active child protection cases, and 35
children receiving case management support in the past week [1119][1118]. Inclusive support reached approximately 80 children with
disabilities on average weekly, with 341 persons with disabilities receiving other support during 11–17 May 2026 [1119][1118]. Partners also
delivered staff-care support to 25 responders during this period [1119][1118][1141].
Partners have repeatedly flagged critical gaps in MHPSS delivery, including shortages of trained caseworkers and MHPSS specialists,
lack of accessible safe spaces and psychosocial materials, fuel and transport constraints, and limited operational funding and
supplies [1119][1118][1141]. At the local level, the Palestinian Women's Development Association integrated psychological and social
support with legal awareness sessions for women in Ein Arik on 12 May 2026, illustrating community-level integration of MHPSS into
protection and legal programming [1175].
Health Needs and Current Response
The most urgent health priorities in Gaza include conflict-driven infectious disease, surge in basic clinical needs, and life-threatening
shortages in chronic care. Nutrition partners recorded a 21% increase in malnutrition admissions between January–April 2026
compared with the same period in 2025 [1125]. Essential chronic-care needs—dialysis for people with kidney failure, insulin for
approximately 11,000 diabetic patients, and treatment for 110 people with haemophilia—face critical shortages, while lack of safe water
and sanitation is driving disease transmission and ectoparasitic outbreaks across displacement sites [1118][1119][1125][1128][1135].
Humanitarian health services in late May included medical evacuations to Egypt, clinical consultations across 181 reporting sites,
infection prevention and control training for over 400 clinical staff and 100+ support staff, and psychosocial/MHPSS support to more
than 10,000 people through partner networks [1118][1119][1141]. WASH-health support remains a central lifesaving activity: 54 partners
deliver about 24,000 m³ of water per day to over 2,000 locations, UNICEF reports reaching up to 1.5 million people with drinking water
depending on access conditions and supporting approximately 1.1 million children with emergency WASH actions, and hygiene and
dignity kit distributions in early-to-mid May benefited almost 116,000 people [1119][1122].
Critical operational gaps undermine response scale-up. The Al Mansoura filling point has been inaccessible since 17 April following the
killing of two UNICEF-contracted drivers, with partners trucking replacement volumes at approximately USD 40,000 per day [1122][1118].
Health facility capacity is severely constrained by attacks, damage and equipment losses, with repeated generator failures
threatening continuity of surgeries, dialysis and intensive care [1123][1128][1158]. Reconstruction and recovery financing remain
inadequate, with UNICEF's 2026 appeal only approximately 36% funded and multilateral reconstruction mechanisms reporting severe
funding shortfalls undermining plans to restore health infrastructure [1128][1133][1139][1122].
Coordination efforts in late May include a 25 May meeting between the Palestinian Prime Minister and the ICRC to expand field
hospitals, facilitate medicine and supply entry, and support evacuations, alongside public calls from UN agencies—UNICEF, WHO,
UNRWA and OCHA—for lifting administrative restrictions on crossings and ensuring entry of fuel, spare parts, water-system
consumables and predictable flexible funding [1145][1139][1125][1119]. Urgent resource shortfalls repeatedly cited by partners include
generators and spare parts, fuel, engine and lubricating oil, water treatment chemicals and reverse-osmosis spares, chlorine and
testing kits [1139][1122].
Nutrition
Availability and Access to Nutrition Services
Widespread acute food insecurity affects roughly 1.6 million people in Gaza (about 77% of the population), including more than 100,000
children and some 37,000 pregnant and lactating women [1186][1190][1197]. Nutrition partners reported a 21% increase in malnutrition
admissions between January–April 2026 compared with the same period in 2025, despite the ceasefire [1189]. Although famine
conditions were reversed, UNICEF notes the number of malnourished and vulnerable children remains extremely serious, with children
who recover at risk of rapid relapse without sufficient clean water and fuel, and there is not a single fully functioning hospital across
Gaza [1192][1196].
Blockades, restrictions on so-called "dual-use" items, and limited crossings constrain the entry of food, therapeutic and medical
supplies, and WASH spare parts, undermining the availability of therapeutic foods, micronutrient supplements, and infant formula
needed for nutrition programmes [1185][1191][1193]. The collapse and damage to health services, widespread shortages of fuel and
medical supplies, and limited functioning hospitals restrict facility-based malnutrition treatment and maternal and newborn nutrition
support, increasing the risk that children who receive treatment will relapse into malnutrition [1220][1189][1192].
Severe water insecurity further undermines feeding and food preparation. UNICEF and partners aim to reach up to 1.5 million people
with drinking water depending on access, yet most families remain water insecure (82%), up to 70% cannot collect the minimum 6
litres per person per day, and desalination output fell from ~20,000 m³/day in March to ~16,000 m³/day due to shortages of chemicals
and spare parts, forcing costlier trucking and reducing capacity to support nutrition and infant feeding needs [1193][1192][1191].
21
The Al Mansoura filling point was rendered inaccessible after the killing of two contracted drivers in mid-April [1192].
Caregivers are adopting negative coping strategies — including mothers reducing their own rations — and maternal malnutrition,
scarce infant formula, and shortages of therapeutic foods and supplements are undermining exclusive breastfeeding and infant and
young child feeding practices, with reports of reduced breastmilk production and reliance on scarce formula and therapeutic
products [1186][1185][1199][1206].
Nutrition Needs and Current Response
As part of May distributions, partners provided general food assistance to 122,000 households (440,000 people), with each family
receiving two parcels — a 25-kg flour bag and 2.5 kg of high-energy biscuits — covering about 75% of minimum caloric needs [1194].
Humanitarian cooking operations have been sharply reduced: World Central Kitchen cut daily cooked meals from approximately 1
million to 200,000 (an 80% reduction), directly affecting roughly 250,000 displaced families dependent on hot meals, with the cuts
attributed to a severe funding crisis and depleted operational resources [1186]. UNOPS delivered over 2.1 million litres of diesel into Gaza
and distributed just over 2 million litres between 7–20 May to support humanitarian operations, yet fuel and access constraints persist
[1194].
Agricultural production has been catastrophically reduced — FAO reports that less than 5% of Gaza's agricultural land remains usable,
and greenhouses and livestock systems were largely destroyed — further undermining local food supply and nutrition recovery
[1217][1202].
Funding shortfalls are acutely constraining the scale-up of nutrition and food assistance. The UN's 2026 flash appeal sought USD 4
billion for 3 million Palestinians in Gaza and the West Bank but had received only about 12% (~USD 490 million) by late May 2026 [1186].
WASH
Availability of Water Sources
As of late May 2026, UNICEF reports average drinking-water availability in Gaza is extremely low, around 4.5–6 litres per person per day,
well below regional norms and household needs [1241][1240]. Most households remain water insecure (82%) and up to 70% are unable
to collect the minimum 6 litres per person per day for drinking and cooking [1240]. UNICEF and partners are sustaining emergency
WASH services by installing water tanks at displacement sites, trucking water from private desalination plants and domestic wells, and
conducting well repairs and pipeline maintenance, reaching up to 1.5 million people with drinking-water distributions and supporting
around 1 million people to access sufficient water for drinking and domestic needs [1241][1240]. Fifty-four partners provide roughly
24,000 m³/day to more than 2,000 locations, with approximately 74% of households relying on such deliveries [1240][1242][1281].
The Al Mansoura filling station has been inaccessible since the 17 April attack that killed two UNICEF-contracted drivers, forcing
partners to truck roughly 2 million litres per day from alternative desalination plants at an additional cost of about USD 40,000 per day
and disrupting daily access for an estimated 285,000 people who relied on it [1240][1267]. Seawater desalination output declined from
approximately 20,000 m³/day in March to approximately 16,000 m³/day due to shortages of chemicals and spare parts [1240][1242].
Severe shortages of fuel, engine oil and spare parts are further disrupting water production and sewage pumping systems—the WASH
Cluster estimates around 7,000 litres of engine oil are required monthly to sustain operations—and partners shut the Sheikh Radwan
stormwater lagoon on 11 May to preserve generator lifespan, increasing flood and public-health risks [1242][1281][1251]. UNICEF's Gaza
hotline logged over 85,000 calls reporting unmet hygiene needs and irregular or insufficient water supply, data which is being used to
adapt WASH programming in accessible areas [1241].
Access to Sanitation Facilities and Hygiene Practices
Sanitation and hygiene in Gaza are under severe strain, with failing sewage systems, widespread rodent and insect infestations, and
acute shortages of fuel, engine oil and spare parts disrupting WASH operations and increasing public-health risks [1240][1242]. UNICEF
supported approximately 508,852 people to access sanitation services in overcrowded conditions, where privacy, cleanliness and safe
waste disposal remain difficult and disease transmission risk is elevated [1241][1240]. UNICEF reached over 603,312 people with critical
WASH supplies, and between 4–17 May distributed 15,343 hygiene kits, 2,448 dignity kits and 7,350 jerry cans benefiting almost 116,000
people [1241][1242][1281].
Menstrual hygiene needs are acute, with reports that nearly 700,000 women and girls in Gaza lack basic sanitary pads, undermining
dignity and safe menstrual management in displacement settings [1269]. Critical maintenance and recovery actions are being
implemented but remain constrained by access and supply restrictions, including emergency repairs to restore wastewater treatment
and network functionality and household pilots for waste segregation that reduced waste volumes by about 15–20% in four shelters
[1241]. Humanitarian actors call for predictable humanitarian access and the entry of fuel, lubricants, chemicals, spare parts, pumps
and equipment, and access to landfills to sustain and scale WASH services and reduce public-health risks [1241][1240].
Waste Management and Vector-Borne Disease Risks
Solid waste is accumulating in and around displacement sites and shelters while managed disposal sites remain inaccessible, leaving
temporary dump sites full or nearly full as of the 29 May 2026 WASH update [1240][1241][1252][1242]. Sanitary landfills near Gaza's
perimeter remain largely inaccessible due to security constraints, and access to debris-removal and heavy equipment has not been
approved, forcing continued reliance on overwhelmed temporary dump sites [1242][1281][1249]. This accumulation is driving severe
environmental health risks: rodent and pest infestations are rising, more than half of households are experiencing skin diseases, and
fleas, lice and scabies are commonplace as of late May 2026, while increasing clinical presentations include respiratory infections and
acute watery diarrhoea [1240][1241][1267][1252].
22
To address pest and rodent infestations, WASH Cluster partners, local organizations and UNDP launched a response plan on 17 May
targeting over 1,700 locations with spraying, rodent control and awareness activities; UNDP brought 3 tonnes of rodenticides and 3,000
litres of pesticides into Gaza, though partners stress that a full response requires access to landfills and approval to bring in
debris-removal and heavy equipment [1242][1281][1251]. The Site Management Cluster reported approximately 1,600 displacement
sites hosting about 1.7 million people (354,480 households) based on data collected between 3 February and 10 May, concentrating
populations in makeshift settings where waste accumulation and pest exposure are greatest [1242][1281]. Kerem Shalom and Zikim
remained the only operational entry points for humanitarian and commercial goods, with offloading rates at 81% between 11 and 17
May, constraining the timely entry of WASH consumables, spare parts and equipment needed to restore sustainable waste
management and reduce vector-borne disease risk [1242][1281].
Main WASH Needs and Humanitarian Response
The main WASH needs in Gaza as of late May 2026 centre on restoring sustainable water supply, rehabilitating sanitation
infrastructure, and scaling hygiene promotion amid severe funding and access constraints. UNICEF's 2026 Humanitarian Action for
Children appeal requires USD 707.8 million, of which only 36% has been funded, leaving USD 451.8 million still needed across sectors
[1241][1240][1251]. Administrative impediments to the entry of water treatment chemicals, spare parts and generator
consumables—alongside attacks on water operations and restricted crossings—continue to undermine the humanitarian response
[1241][1240].
On the operational side, emergency effectiveness has been maintained through fuel supplies, urgent repairs and temporary
wastewater treatment restorations. However, scaling and sustainability remain constrained. Desalination output has declined due to
chemical and spare-part shortages, and the estimated monthly shortfall of approximately 7,000 litres of engine oil threatens
generator-dependent systems including water production, trucking and sewage pumping [1240][1242][1281]. The shutdown of the
Sheikh Radwan stormwater lagoon on 11 May to preserve generator lifespan caused water and wastewater levels to rise significantly,
increasing flood risk [1242][1251].
Humanitarian partners continue to deliver emergency services where access allows: water trucking, desalination support, well repairs
and pipeline maintenance collectively reach millions of people, while hygiene kit distributions address acute NFI gaps [1242][1281][1241].
Actors stress the urgent need for predictable humanitarian access, the unimpeded entry of fuel, chemicals, spare parts and
equipment, and access to sanitary landfills to sustain and expand life-saving WASH services across Gaza [1241][1240][1251].
Shelter and Non-Food Items (NFI)
Damage to Housing and Shelter Needs
Humanitarian partners report massive housing loss in Gaza, with roughly 1.7 million people sheltering in about 1,600 displacement
sites—primarily overcrowded tents, schools, or damaged structures [1307][1365][1326]. Estimates indicate approximately 1.2 million
homes have been lost, with up to 60 percent of the population having lost their homes completely, and large residential complexes
such as Hamad City reduced to rubble [1321][1317]. Community infrastructure has been severely affected: schools and UN facilities are
being used as shelters, hospitals and markets have been damaged or destroyed, and entire residential blocks and public housing
projects have been razed, compounding the loss of habitual community spaces and complicating return or reconstruction
[1314][1317][1308]. UNRWA and UNICEF reported that evacuation and shelter-in-place orders have affected shelters and at least two
UNRWA installations, including a school-turned-shelter in Nuseirat, while overcrowding, poor sanitation, rodent infestations, and
outbreaks of skin and diarrhoeal diseases are undermining shelter habitability [1314][1319][1316]. Between 16–17 May, partners recorded
displacement of more than 150 families from eastern Khan Younis and eastern Gaza City, of whom 40 families were identified as
requiring emergency assistance, and an 18 May airstrike reportedly damaged tents and tarpaulins belonging to 35 families in Jabalya
Camp [1306][1313]. In the West Bank, rebuilding and durable shelter solutions are constrained by administrative restrictions and limits
on entry of materials imposed by Israeli authorities, difficulties in obtaining Israeli-issued building permits linked to demolitions in Area
C, and settlement expansion plans that threaten forcible displacement of communities such as Khan al-Ahmar [1307][1306][1334].
Priority shelter interventions identified by partners include immediate NFI and cash support and provision of safe, durable alternatives
in hotspots such as eastern Khan Younis, eastern Gaza City, Jabalya Camp, and West Bank Area C communities facing demolitions
[1306][1307].
Living Conditions in Camps or Settlements
Displacement sites across Gaza are severely overcrowded, offering limited privacy and dignity, particularly for women and girls. CARE
and partner reporting indicates nearly 700,000 women and girls in Gaza lack basic sanitary pads, compounding protection and health
risks in crowded settings [1344][1316]. Water insecurity is acute: UNICEF reports 82% of families remain water insecure and up to 70%
cannot collect the minimum 6 litres per person per day for drinking and cooking [1319]. The Al Mansoura filling point has been
inaccessible since the 17 April killing of two UNICEF-contracted drivers, forcing costlier trucking of water at approximately USD 40,000
per day to replace roughly 2 million litres per day previously sourced there, while seawater desalination output fell from 20,000 m³/day
in March to 16,000 m³/day due to shortages of chemicals and spare parts [1319][1307]. Fuel and lubricant shortages are further
disrupting water production and sewage pumping, contributing to shutdowns such as the Sheikh Radwan stormwater lagoon on 11
May [1319][1307]. Solid waste is accumulating at displacement sites as temporary dump sites reach or near capacity and sanitary
landfills remain inaccessible, with humanitarian partners reporting increasing rodent and pest infestations entering shelters and
contaminating food and living spaces [1319][1316][1314]. These deteriorating conditions have direct health and nutrition consequences,
including rises in acute watery diarrhoea, respiratory and ectoparasitic infections such as scabies and rodent bites, and a 21% increase
in malnutrition admissions between January–April 2026 compared to the same period in 2025 [1314].
23
Ongoing hostilities continue to worsen displacement conditions, with strikes in mid-May damaging tents and makeshift
shelters—including an 18 May incident in Jabalya Camp—and airstrikes near humanitarian facilities also reported around Eid on 26
May [1306][1323].
NFI and Shelter Needs and Current Response
Between 11–17 May, partners provided 14,429 households with shelter and non-food assistance, including 14,936 bedding items, 3,173
bedding kits, 2,922 tarpaulins, 1,114 sealing-off kits, and 839 clothing kits; the Rapid Joint Distribution Mechanism supported 55
households with 10 tent packages and 45 sealing-off kits, and partners installed 115 emergency shelters in Gaza and Khan Younis
[1306]. Between 1 January and 30 April, the Shelter Cluster reached over 9,300 households (more than 40,300 people) across the West
Bank with shelter repairs, cash for rent, tents, plastic sheeting, bedding kits, kitchen sets, and clothing vouchers [1307][1306]. UNICEF
reported reaching over 603,300 people with critical WASH and hygiene NFI supplies, with drinking-water distributions reaching up to 1.5
million people in late May, though core water systems remain under severe strain [1316][1319]. Despite these efforts, available stocks of
essential household items were reported as sufficient for fewer than 3,400 additional households, and partners flagged administrative
impediments imposed by Israeli authorities restricting entry of shelter items and limiting the scale-up of distributions [1306].
Humanitarian logistics and funding constraints are further limiting NFI delivery: only about half of aid trucks from Egypt offloaded in the
first 18 days of May, Kerem Shalom/Karem Abu Salem remains the main cargo crossing, and engine oil and fuel shortages are
disrupting WASH and shelter operations [1326][1311][1325]. Severe funding shortfalls compound these challenges, with the UN 2026
appeal approximately 12% funded at the time of reporting, forcing cuts that hamper both NFI and shelter responses [1325].
Protection
Impact on civilians
Between 12 and 18 May, Israeli forces and settlers killed five Palestinians (including one child) and injured nearly 60 across the West
Bank [1382]. OCHA documented more than 50 settler attacks that week, noting an average of six such attacks per day in 2026 and over
870 such attacks across more than 220 communities since the start of the year [1382]. OCHA also documented more than 40 raids and
other operations by Israeli forces across the West Bank during the reporting period, including prolonged raids in Tubas, Salfit, Jenin and
Nablus and a 19-hour closure of Burin village on 17 May that disrupted movement, work and education [1396]. Approximately 100
students attempting to reach a Palestinian school in Hebron's H2 area faced repeated delays and restrictive checks at As Salaymeh
(160) checkpoint on 11, 14 and 18 May, with 103 students unable to reach the school on 11 May [1396]. Demolitions and confiscation
operations continued in late May, including home demolitions in Rantis on 19 May and further demolitions and seizures in Silwan and
multiple firing zones on 25 May, displacing families and destroying livelihoods and infrastructure [1379]. Israeli moves to revive
demolition orders and, on 1 June, to invite bids for 3,400 settlement units in the West Bank threaten imminent forced displacement for
communities including Khan al-Ahmar, putting around 4,000 Palestinians across 18 communities at risk of losing homes and land
[1433][1393].
In Gaza, renewed escalation in late May caused new displacement: more than 150 families fled eastern Khan Younis and eastern Gaza
City on 16–17 May, of whom 40 families were identified as requiring emergency assistance after fleeing with only what they could carry
[1382]. An airstrike on 18 May reportedly damaged tents and tarpaulins used by 35 families in Jabalya Camp, displacing dozens [1382].
UNRWA reported renewed military escalation and shelter-in-place orders affecting UNRWA installations and displacement sites
between 20–25 May [1393]. Partners and WHO reported rising ectoparasitic infections, rodent bites, chickenpox and acute watery
diarrhoea in Gaza, and WHO warned that dire conditions and blockages of medical supplies are worsening public-health risks [1393].
Only half of aid trucks from Egypt could offload at Kerem Shalom in the first 18 days of May, and partners launched a pest-control
campaign in over 1,700 locations while warning that shortages and access restrictions limit the response [1382]. These raids, closures,
evacuation and shelter-in-place orders are disrupting access to education, health care and livelihoods — forcing school closures and
exam postponements, shutting shops, and interrupting work and services [1396]. Shelter actors reached over 9,300 households (more
than 40,300 people) across the West Bank with shelter assistance between 1 January and 30 April, including repairs, tents, cash for rent
and protective measures [1396].
Factors Driving protection risks
Escalating settler violence and Israeli forces' operations across the West Bank have driven acute protection risks in late May 2026, with
more than 50 settler attacks documented in one week and an average of six attacks per day in 2026 [1382]. Concerns over forced
displacement intensified after Israeli authorities moved to implement long-standing demolition orders in Area C and East Jerusalem,
notably affecting Khan al-Ahmar; OCHA records approximately 550 structures demolished in 18 Bedouin and herding communities
since 2009 — including 175 donor-funded structures — heightening the risk of community-level forcible displacement [1396].
In Gaza, recurrent strikes, shelling and military operations have kept large numbers displaced in overcrowded tents and damaged
shelters, while humanitarian access is constrained by logistics and crossing restrictions [1382]. UNRWA and WHO reported worsening
public-health and protection threats in late May, including rising ectoparasitic infections and rodent infestations, linked to blockages
of medical supplies and constrained services [1393]. A recent UN report documenting conflict-related sexual violence and the March
2026 dropping of charges in emblematic abuse cases point to systemic accountability gaps in detention and interrogation settings,
increasing risks of torture, sexual violence and impunity for detainees [1398]. Movement restrictions and checkpoint procedures —
including repeated delays and denial of access for students in Hebron in May and requirements for humanitarian teams to coordinate
movements with Israeli authorities — impede access to education, services and assistance [1396][1382]. Explosive remnants and
explosive-ordnance risks remain a protection hazard for children and communities, prompting regular explosive-ordnance risk
education and psychosocial responses by protection partners [1382][1396].
24
Psychological and mental health impacts
Between 11 and 17 May, humanitarian partners delivered mental health and psychosocial support (MHPSS) — including psychological
first aid and individual and group counselling — reaching 8,259 people according to Protection Cluster reporting, contributing to a
broader protection response that reached more than 10,000 people in the same period [1382][1410]. Child protection partners provided
MHPSS on a weekly average to about 1,600 children (including roughly 80 children with disabilities) and more than 670 caregivers,
alongside awareness sessions and case management for a smaller number of children [1382][1396]. Services included recreational
activities, art and drama sessions, counselling and parenting support delivered in shelters, camps, schools and displacement sites
[1410][1382].
Repeated displacement, overcrowding in shelters, loss of homes and exposure to ongoing strikes are generating high levels of chronic
stress, anxiety and trauma — particularly among children who face disrupted schooling, disrupted routines and repeated exposure to
violence [1376][1402][1384][1435]. Partners stressed that fuel, safe spaces, trained staff and basic resources are required to sustain
these interventions. Protection partners also provided limited staff-care activities, with 25 responders receiving staff care during the
referenced week, noting psychological distress among front-line staff alongside constraints caused by access restrictions and
resource shortages [1382].
Protection Needs and Current Response
Between 11–17 May, protection partners delivered lifesaving and protection-related services to almost 11,000 people, including legal aid
to 219 people, referrals and case follow-up to 232 people, support to 341 persons with disabilities, group psychosocial sessions to 350
people, mine-action and explosive ordnance risk education to 168 people, relief distributed to 1,265 people, and staff care for 25
responders [1382]. During the same period, partners assisted 146 returnees from Egypt, and since the reopening of the Rafah border
and as of 14 May, 2,429 returnees had been assisted, including 885 currently receiving follow-up protection services [1382]. Protection
monitoring continued through eight focus group discussions and 130 key-informant interviews across 16 neighbourhoods, reaching
1,155 people [1382].
Shelter-related protective measures included the installation of 115 emergency shelters in Gaza and Khan Younis and provision of
shelter and non-food assistance to 14,429 households, while available stocks remain sufficient for fewer than 3,400 additional
households and administrative impediments continue to restrict shelter items [1382][1396]. OCHA reported an airstrike near five
humanitarian facilities in Deir al-Balah and reiterated calls for opening more crossings and for fuel, safe spaces, staff and other basic
resources to sustain protection and child-focused services [1410][1382].
The Palestinian Prisoners' Club warned of scabies outbreaks and alleged denial of care in Israeli prisons, and UN officials verified 31
cases of sexual abuse in Israeli prisons in 2025, prompting calls for immediate access by lawyers, medical personnel and the ICRC and
for accountability measures [1402][1424][1434][1411]. Medical Aid for Palestinians reported cumulative Gaza deaths exceeding 72,700
since October 2023, including more than 1,700 healthcare workers and 594 aid workers, which undermines protection of civilians and
humanitarian access [1446]. Humanitarian actors reported key operational gaps constraining protection work, including acute
psychological distress among staff, persistent fuel shortages, rising transportation costs, cash-flow constraints, and limited availability
of essential materials and spare parts [1382].
Child Protection concerns
Between 11 and 17 May, child protection partners provided individual case management to 56 newly identified high-risk children,
continued follow-up for more than 3,000 active child protection cases, carried out 135 follow-up contacts for unaccompanied and
separated children to assess wellbeing and care arrangements, and delivered child-protection awareness and community-based
protection activities reaching about 4,700 children and caregivers [1382][1396]. On a weekly average, child protection partners reach
approximately 380 children and 100 caregivers through awareness and explosive-ordnance risk education, and provided cash or
in-kind assistance to about 60 children and 12 caregivers, while 35 children received case management support [1396]. Partners report
that child-friendly spaces, high-risk case management and MHPSS services face disruptions and urgently require additional funding,
fuel, transport, operational supplies, trained case workers and accessible safe spaces [1382][1396].
The Palestinian Centre for Missing and Enforcedly Hidden Persons reported an estimated 2,900 missing or forcibly hidden children in
Gaza — approximately 2,700 believed to be under rubble — and called for urgent international action on 25 May to determine their fate
[1411]. The UN verified multiple incidents of conflict-related sexual violence affecting children in the occupied Palestinian territory,
including cases involving nine boys and one girl from Gaza and the West Bank, with many incidents occurring during detention and
interrogation [1404][1398].
Humanitarian and court data indicate a rise in early marriage as a negative coping mechanism. Supreme Shariah Court figures for
2024–2025 show that 20.6% of 35,474 recorded marriages involved girls under 18, including 627 marriages of girls under 15, reversing
earlier declines noted in 2022 [1387][1399].
Violence against women and girls
The United Nations verified 31 cases of conflict-related sexual violence affecting Palestinians from Gaza and the West Bank, involving 14
men, seven women, nine boys and one girl, with 13 cases recorded in 2025 and 18 dating to 2023–2024 [1398][1434][1395]. Documented
violations included rape (including with objects), gang rape, attempted rape, targeted physical violence to genitals (including
shootings), forced nudity, degrading strip and cavity searches, unwanted touching, threats of rape, and in some instances filmed or
photographed abuse [1398][1395][1450]. The UN attributed responsibility to members of the Israeli military, the Israel Prison Service
(including the Keter unit) and police counter-terrorism units (Yamam), noting that most abuses occurred during detention and
interrogation across multiple sites including Sde Teiman, Etzion and several prisons and police stations [1398][1395][1386].
25
The report highlighted persistent barriers to reporting and investigation — including denial of UN and ICRC access to detention
facilities, threats against detainees that deter reporting, and a systemic lack of accountability — and noted that charges in the
emblematic Sde Teiman case were dropped in March 2026, a development the UN warned could entrench impunity [1386][1390][1395].
OCHA and protection partners warned that settler and military-related violence is compounding GBV risks and driving displacement
across the West Bank, documenting over 870 settler attacks in 2026 up to mid-May that heighten protection needs for women and
girls [1382][1396]. Local women's organisations continue legal-awareness and protection programming in the West Bank, for example
a SPEED project session in Ein Arik on 12 May 2026 to strengthen legal knowledge and link psychosocial support with access to justice
[1467].
Vulnerable Groups / Special Needs Groups
Unaccompanied and Separated Children
Between 11 and 17 May, partners provided mental health and psychosocial support (MHPSS) to over 4,000 children and about 1,500
caregivers, and carried out child protection awareness and community-based activities reaching approximately 4,700 children and
caregivers across shelters, camps, schools and displacement sites [1484][1483]. Partners provided individual case management to 56
newly identified high-risk children, continued follow-up for more than 3,000 active child-protection cases involving children without
parental care, and conducted 135 follow-up contacts specifically for unaccompanied and separated children to assess wellbeing,
care arrangements and protection concerns [1484][1483]. Partners state that sustaining high-risk case management and MHPSS
services is contingent on additional funding, fuel, transport and operational supplies, as well as more trained caseworkers, MHPSS
specialists, accessible safe spaces and psychosocial materials [1484][1483]. The Palestinian Centre for Missing and Enforcedly Hidden
Persons reported some 2,900 children in Gaza as missing or forcibly hidden, including an estimate that around 2,700 remain under
rubble or unaccounted for, and called for urgent action to determine their fate (campaign issued around 25 May) [1501].
Pregnant and Lactating Women
A January 2026 report by Physicians for Human Rights and the Global Human Rights Clinic at the University of Chicago concluded that
attacks on reproductive health facilities, severe restrictions on medical and food supplies, and widespread malnutrition in 2025
contributed to increased miscarriages, maternal and neonatal deaths, infertility, and long-term reproductive harms while severely
undermining access to basic maternal and neonatal care [1486]. The World Food Programme estimated that 1.6 million people in Gaza
face high levels of acute food insecurity, including approximately 37,000 pregnant and lactating women, increasing risks of maternal
malnutrition and reduced breastmilk production [1496]. Field reporting and UNICEF further noted that maternal malnutrition and lack of
access to prenatal care have reduced breastfeeding capacity and that formula and routine pregnancy check-ups are often
unavailable [1502][1500]. UNRWA reported a 21% increase in malnutrition admissions between January–April 2026 compared with the
same period in 2025, signaling rising nutritional risks for mothers and newborns [1485]. UNICEF warned on 29 May that severe water
insecurity, fuel shortages and restrictions on spare parts and chemicals have reduced desalination and trucking capacity, limiting
families' access to water needed for cooking, hygiene and infant care [1492]. Between 4–17 May, UNICEF distributed 15,343 hygiene kits,
2,448 dignity kits and 7,350 jerry cans, benefiting almost 116,000 people [1484]. Partners supported medical evacuations of 59 patients
(including six children) to Egypt via Rafah between 11–17 May, and on 25 May the Prime Minister met ICRC representatives to discuss
scaling field hospitals, facilitating entry of medicines and expanding evacuations for wounded Palestinians, indicating constrained but
ongoing efforts to restore maternal health services [1484][1541]. The Palestinian Ministry of Women's Affairs and UN bodies warned that
occupation-related violence, displacement and the collapse of services have increased risks of gender-based violence and limited
survivors' access to protection and specialized services [1516].
Adolescents and Youth
Students faced access restrictions to education in the West Bank: about 100 students attempting to reach a Palestinian school in
Hebron's H2 area were repeatedly delayed at the As Salaymeh checkpoint, and on 11 May 103 students were unable to reach the school,
with similar disruptions reported on 14 and 18 May [1483]. UNRWA opened a Temporary Learning Space at a displacement site south of
Khan Younis on 20 May and registered 1,600 children within a week [1485]. Community-based psychosocial support remains the
primary youth intervention, complemented by recreational and structured activities and parenting sessions [1483][1484]. Between 11–17
May, protection partners provided MHPSS to 8,259 people, while child protection actors provided MHPSS to over 4,000 children and
reached about 1,500 caregivers [1483][1484]. Graduates and young jobseekers in the West Bank face a collapsed job market, with
public-sector wage disruptions and rising unemployment prompting many to seek low-paid work or leave, according to Al Jazeera
reporting [1508].
People with Disabilities
Child protection partners provide MHPSS to approximately 1,600 children weekly, including about 80 children with disabilities, and to
more than 670 caregivers [1483][1484]. Between 11 and 17 May, 11 protection partners delivered MHPSS to 8,259 people, provided legal
aid to 219 people, referrals and case follow-up to 232 people, and provided other support to 341 persons with disabilities, while 35
children received case management support including specialized referrals [1483][1484].
Elderly
Access to healthcare, essential medicines and regular treatment for chronic conditions is critically disrupted in Gaza, with the Gaza
Ministry of Health warning that 250 dialysis patients risk losing access and 11,000 diabetic patients face insulin shortages amid a
collapsing health system and blockages of medical supplies reported by UN agencies and WHO [1509][1488][1485]. Repeated
displacement, evacuation orders and movement restrictions have sharply limited older people's ability to reach care and services,
particularly in areas subject to evacuation or siege-like conditions and in isolated West Bank communities cut off by checkpoints and
26
settlement expansion [1486][1484][1559]. Overcrowded, unsanitary shelters and inadequate WASH and waste management—reported
across displacement sites—are increasing health risks for older people and those with limited mobility, while available shelter sites
generally lack age-friendly adaptations and privacy measures [1484][1498][1492]. Recurrent displacement and the destruction of
homes have eroded social networks and community supports that older people rely on for care and intergenerational assistance,
undermining their social protection and dignity [1496][1491]. Older Palestinians detained in Israeli custody have been reported as
facing denial of healthcare, malnutrition and other abusive treatment, a protection concern raised by Palestinian officials and UN
investigations into detention abuses [1541][1531]. Humanitarian partners are providing targeted support that can benefit older
people—such as mental health and psychosocial services, assistance to persons with disabilities, mobile clinics and shelter
repairs—but coverage gaps and constrained access mean many older people remain unreached [1483][1484][1559]. Sustaining
services for older people depends on urgent unimpeded delivery of fuel, medical supplies, spare parts and safe humanitarian access,
needs repeatedly highlighted by UNICEF and OCHA as critical to keep health, water and social services functioning [1492][1484].
Displaced
UN and rights bodies warned in late May that nearly the entire Gaza population remains displaced and concentrated into an
increasingly narrow area following multiple displacement orders, and that recent announcements to expand Israeli forces' control of
Gaza territory raise grave concerns about civilians' ability to find safety and access humanitarian assistance [1494][1509][1521].
Between 16 and 17 May, humanitarian partners recorded the displacement of more than 150 families from eastern Khan Younis and
eastern Gaza City, of which 40 families were identified as requiring emergency assistance [1484]. Many displaced families are
sheltering in overcrowded tents, schools or damaged structures with few safe alternatives amid recurrent strikes and access
constraints disrupting humanitarian activities [1484][1483]. Shelter Cluster partners reached over 9,300 households (more than 40,300
people) across the West Bank with shelter assistance between 1 January and 30 April 2026, and partners installed 115 emergency
shelters in Gaza and Khan Younis, but available stocks were reported as sufficient for fewer than 3,400 additional households
[1484][1483]. UNICEF reported in late May 2026 that emergency WASH services are being sustained through trucking, desalination and
tank installations, supporting around 1 million people with sufficient drinking water at scale and reaching up to 1.5 million people with
drinking-water distributions, while noting that 82% of families remain water insecure and up to 70% cannot collect the minimum six
litres per person per day [1498][1492]. UNICEF also reported delivering water tanks to shelters, providing critical hygiene items to over
603,300 people and supporting nearly 509,000 people with sanitation services, while warning of mounting rodent and pest infestations
and inaccessible landfills that increase public-health risks [1498][1492]. UNRWA and WHO reported rising infectious-disease and
nutrition concerns in May 2026, with UNRWA noting increased ectoparasitic infections and WHO warning that blockages of essential
medical supplies are worsening outbreaks and service collapse [1485]. Protection partners provided lifesaving protection and MHPSS
to almost 11,000 people between 11–17 May and assisted 2,429 returnees via Rafah as of 14 May, with 885 currently receiving follow-up
protection services [1484]. In the West Bank, demolition orders and settlement expansion are heightening displacement risks: Khan
al-Ahmar and 17 other Bedouin/herding communities (around 4,000 people) face imminent forced displacement, and OCHA has
documented roughly 550 structure demolitions in those communities since 2009, including 175 donor-funded structures
[1484][1559][1483]. Escalating settler violence and demolitions have caused repeated local displacements and obstructed access to
livelihoods and services, with reports documenting more than 50 settler attacks in a single week and a 2026 total of some 870
incidents across over 220 communities [1509].
Minorities
On 16 May, 22 Palestinian Bedouin families (137 people) were forcibly displaced from the Jiljiliya area following a large settler attack;
those families had previously been displaced from other communities in 2023 [1483]. Israeli authorities moved to revive demolition
orders against Khan al-Ahmar and scheduled a 1 June tender for 3,400 settlement units in the E1 area, putting Khan al-Ahmar among
18 Bedouin and herding communities — around 4,000 people — at heightened risk of forced displacement [1559][1546]. Escalating
settler violence across the West Bank — more than 50 attacks in one week and an average of six attacks per day in 2026 — has
resulted in casualties, property damage and increased protection risks for Palestinian communities [1484]. The UN Secretary-General's
annual report placed Israeli occupation forces on the list of parties credibly suspected of patterns of conflict-related sexual violence;
UN investigators verified multiple cases affecting men, women and children, noting many abuses occurred during detention and
interrogation and highlighting a systemic lack of accountability [1531][1538]. Local women's organisations have begun legal awareness
and access-to-justice sessions — including a Palestinian Women's Development Association session on 12 May 2026 as part of a
72-session series — to strengthen legal protection for women [1483][1565]. Humanitarian partners warn that access constraints and
restricted aid deliveries limit the effectiveness of protection and inclusion efforts for minority and marginalised groups across the
Occupied Palestinian Territory [1484][1483].
27
Governorates
West Bank
Ramallah & Al-Bireh
During the week of 12–18 May 2026, Ramallah & Al-Bireh governorate experienced a severe escalation of settler violence and military
operations, with multiple communities directly affected. According to OCHA's 25 May 2026 Humanitarian Situation Report, a
large-scale attack on 13 May across Sinjil, Jiljiliya, and Abwein villages in Ramallah governorate resulted in Israeli forces and settlers
shooting and killing one Palestinian and injuring 10 others. Dozens of Israeli settlers raided the western area of Sinjil and nearby areas,
stealing Palestinian-owned livestock and other property. When residents attempted to retrieve stolen flocks, Israeli forces and settlers
fired live ammunition, rubber bullets, and tear gas canisters [1589][1590]. The consequences were severe: on 16 May, 22 Palestinian
Bedouin families comprising 137 people, including 81 children, were forcibly displaced from the area following recurrent settler attacks
and intimidation. These families had previously been displaced from three other communities in 2023 due to settler violence,
highlighting their acute and compounding vulnerability [1589][1590].
Arson attacks also escalated significantly across Ramallah governorate during the reporting period. Israeli settlers set fire to a mosque
in Jibiya village in Ramallah governorate, also torching a Palestinian-owned car and spray-painting Hebrew graffiti on the mosque
walls [1590][1589]. In Al Mughayyir and Burqa villages in Ramallah governorate, settlers set fire to agricultural land, burning olive trees
and cultivated areas, with one fire spreading across approximately 10 dunums due to strong winds [1590][1589]. The village of
Al-Mughayyir, northeast of Ramallah, also saw additional raids and arrests by Israeli forces in the following days: on 21 May, Israeli
forces set fire to farmland west of the village using tear gas canisters and fired on locals who tried to extinguish the flames [1592]. In an
early morning raid on 22 May, approximately 20 soldiers beat an activist, punching him in the face and whipping him with a belt, while
ransacking four homes and arresting children in Al-Mughayyir [1592].
Settlers from a recently established outpost near Ein 'Arik village in Ramallah governorate reportedly raided homes, physically
assaulted four Palestinians, vandalized water tanks and construction materials, and seized electric cables [1590][1589]. In a separate
incident, settlers attacked Palestinian families in the Khalifa area of the village of Tayya'a, east of Ramallah, deliberately bringing sheep
and camels near homes, causing destruction of property and trees, and surrounding Palestinian residences during the attack [1583].
Two homes in Rantis, west of Ramallah, were demolished without warning by Israeli forces on 19 May, displacing families with no time
to collect their belongings — one home housed a woman and her son, and the other a family of nine including seven children [1592].
Additionally, Israeli forces demolished a cement factory in Kharbatha Bani Harith and a house in Shuqba, both in Ramallah
governorate [1592].
On 12 May, Israeli forces opened fire toward two Palestinians attempting to scale the Barrier near Dahiyat al Bareed in Jerusalem
governorate, killing a Palestinian man originally from Deir Qaddis village in Ramallah governorate and injuring another, as they
reportedly attempted to cross to access work in Israel [1590][1589].
Israeli forces also carried out a raid in Deir Jarir village in Ramallah governorate on 27 May, resulting in the detention of a villager [1601].
On the protection front, the Palestinian Women's Working Development Association organized a legal awareness-raising meeting for 15
women in Ein Arik village, west of Ramallah, on 12 May 2026, within the SPEED project aimed at strengthening protection, psychosocial
support, and emergency response services for women and girls. The session covered legal rights including personal status,
inheritance, custody, and procedures for recovering official documents lost due to displacement or demolition [1617].
In August 2025, Israel announced the inclusion of three Palestinian archaeological sites in Ramallah governorate on its list of "Israeli
archaeological sites," as part of a broader West Bank-wide reclassification effort [1595]. Separately, the village of Al-Nabi Samuel,
situated between Ramallah and Jerusalem, was affected by an Israeli Civil Administration order confiscating approximately 28 acres
of land around the Prophet Samuel site, including areas of the historic mosque and surrounding towns, under the declared pretext of a
development project for archaeological preservation [1579].
The national and Islamic forces of Ramallah and Al-Bireh governorate publicly called for the expansion of popular activities in support
of prisoners and human rights institutions, emphasizing resistance to occupation plans including settler outpost expansion and the
displacement of Palestinian communities [1582].
Hebron
In the Hebron governorate, the reporting period of 12–18 May 2026 saw a significant escalation of settler violence, security incidents,
and access restrictions with direct humanitarian implications for Palestinian communities.
On 17 May, Israeli forces shot a Palestinian man near the Barrier in Beit Ula village, in Hebron governorate, while he was reportedly
attempting to cross. He succumbed to his wounds the following day, according to OCHA [1590][1589].
Settler violence was particularly pronounced across the governorate during this period. In Wadi ar Rakhim community near Susiya, in
southern Hebron governorate, Israeli settlers threw flammable materials toward a Palestinian home, setting fire to an external kitchen,
damaging a parked vehicle, and causing damage to parts of the house [1590][1589]. In a separate incident on 17 May in Umm ad
Daraj community near Sa'ir village, a large group of settlers reportedly physically assaulted four Palestinians and damaged residential
structures and personal property [1590][1589]. Additionally, settlers attacked Palestinian farmland and damaged several trees in
Khirbet el-Muraq in Masafer Yatta, south of Hebron [1610]. According to UN reports, escalating settler violence in southern Hebron and
the Jordan Valley has reportedly led to the total displacement of a number of communities since the outbreak of the war, with settlers
taking control of their territories [1579].
28
In 2025, around 17 Israeli settlers attacked and destroyed a clinic in Hebron governorate, according to the Safeguarding Health in
Conflict Coalition (SHCC), representing a severe attack on health infrastructure [1607].
Access to education in Hebron city is also severely disrupted. About 100 students attempting to reach a Palestinian school in the H2
area of Hebron city through As Salaymeh (160) checkpoint were repeatedly subjected to delays and restrictive measures by Israeli
forces, including demands to present birth certificates and, in some cases, be accompanied by a parent. On 11 May, 103 students were
unable to reach the school altogether, and similar restrictions were again reported on 14 and 18 May [1590][1589].
Movement restrictions in Hebron governorate are particularly severe. The governorate is experiencing frequent closures of major and
sub-barriers, as well as tighter procedures at the entrances to Bethlehem townships and around the Gush Etzion settlement complex,
exacerbating the suffering of the population and resulting in partial paralysis of movement [1586]. An operation near the Gush Etzion
junction resulted in the killing of a 30-year-old resident of Hebron city by Israeli forces [1586].
During Eid al-Adha, Israeli forces closed the gates of the Ibrahimi Mosque in the occupied city of Hebron and fired sound bombs at
worshippers, with attendance estimated at only 300 people — no more than 30% of the usual number, according to the Governor of
Hebron. The mosque, located in the Old City of Hebron where approximately 400 settlers are housed and guarded by roughly 1,500
Israeli soldiers, has faced increasing access restrictions in recent years [1584]. The Ibrahimi Mosque is also among sites at risk under a
proposed Israeli heritage authority bill, which Palestinian and human rights organizations fear could be used to redefine sovereignty
and historical identity [1595].
