The Palestinian Ministry of Women's Affairs, represented by Hebron officials, highlighted that Israeli aggression, forced displacement,
and destruction of infrastructure have increased risks faced by women and girls and created conditions of heightened vulnerability to
gender-based violence, including sexual violence, exploitation, and economic exclusion [1596].
Jenin
During the reporting period of 25 May to 1 June 2026, Jenin governorate continued to face severe humanitarian conditions driven by
ongoing Israeli military operations, movement restrictions, and violence affecting civilians, health care, and essential services.
On 16 May, Israeli forces shot and killed a Palestinian man at the entrance to Jenin Camp, reportedly while he was attempting to enter,
according to OCHA. Jenin Camp has remained a closed military zone since January 2025. On 26 May, Israeli forces fatally shot another
Palestinian man in Jenin refugee camp during a raid, after he sustained wounds to the lower half of his body. On 27 May, Israeli
occupation forces raided Qabatiya town in Jenin governorate, deploying infantry units, breaking into several homes, and detaining
three residents. On the same day, Israeli soldiers denied Palestinian families access to ash-Shuhada Cemetery on the outskirts of Jenin
refugee camp, firing barrages of tear gas canisters at Palestinians attempting to visit graves of their relatives.
During the broader reporting period, OCHA documented prolonged raids in Jenin, among other governorates including Tubas, Salfit,
and Nablus, involving large-scale house searches, temporary takeover of Palestinian homes for military use, detentions, and reported
physical assaults.
The humanitarian situation in Jenin has been deeply affected since January 2025, when the IDF carried out a major security operation
in Jenin refugee camp, causing extensive damage to public and private infrastructure and large-scale displacement of residents.
Following this operation, several health clinics in Jenin camp were forced to temporarily suspend services or faced significant
operational challenges, further constraining access to health care. In January 2025, the IDF also took over Jenin Hospital in Jenin
refugee camp and turned it into a military barracks, arresting nurses who were treating injured people. Explosive weapons, including
drone-fired missiles and controlled demolitions, damaged a hospital in Jenin. Palestinian Authority security forces also violently raided
al-Razi Hospital in Jenin city and arrested a wounded patient on accusations related to Jenin Brigade membership.
Humanitarian access in northern West Bank, where Jenin is located, has been particularly severely constrained, with military
operations damaging infrastructure and disrupting health care and other critical services throughout 2025 and into 2026. According to
WHO, 25% of scheduled appointments across the West Bank were missed because of movement restrictions, a trend that is especially
acute in conflict-affected northern areas such as Jenin where hospitals and health centers were forced to close or suspend
operations.
Nablus
In the reporting period covering late May 2026, Nablus governorate experienced significant escalation in Israeli military operations,
settler violence, and protection incidents with direct humanitarian implications.
On 14 May, Israeli forces shot and killed a Palestinian child in Al Lubban ash Sharqiya village in Nablus governorate and withheld his
body. According to OCHA, the Israeli military stated that soldiers opened fire near Road 60 after stones were thrown at Israeli vehicles
[1590][1589].
Israeli forces carried out prolonged raids in Nablus governorate involving large-scale house searches, temporary takeover of
Palestinian homes for military use, detentions, and reported physical assaults [1589][1590]. On 17 May, Israeli forces closed all
entrances to Burin village — a community of approximately 3,000 Palestinians southwest of Nablus city — for nearly 19 hours, reportedly
after alleging stones had been thrown at Israeli vehicles. The closure disrupted movement and access to work and education, forcing
shops to close and leading some schools to postpone exams and suspend classes [1589][1590]. On 24 May, Israeli soldiers detained
more than a dozen residents in Burin; all but one were later released, reportedly after being beaten by Israeli forces [1592].
Settler violence in Burin village was also documented during the same period. At least two attacks on homes in Burin included
attempted break-ins, property damage, and physical assaults against residents. In one incident on 13 May, a 13-year-old Palestinian
girl was struck on the head with a stick by an Israeli settler during an attack on her family's home while her family attempted to protect
29
their livestock. She was treated at the scene by Palestine Red Crescent Society paramedics [1589][1590].
Israeli settlers also carried out multiple assaults on Palestinian homes and infrastructure across Nablus governorate, including physical
assaults against Palestinians, attacks on homes while families including children were inside, damage to water and electricity
infrastructure, theft and vandalism of agricultural property, and destruction of olive trees and fencing [1589][1590]. Separately, settlers
attacked homes in Beita, south of Nablus city, throwing stones and smashing vehicles [1610].
On 27 May, Israeli forces raided Tell and Beit Iba towns in Nablus governorate, ransacking several homes and detaining four occupants
[1601]. On 28 May, Israeli forces raided towns south and west of Nablus and arrested four Palestinians after storming and searching
several homes [1604]. On 29 May, Israeli forces detained a man and a woman during a dawn raid on Al-Ein refugee camp west of
Nablus, raiding and searching several homes, vandalizing property, and detaining two Palestinians [1577]. That same night, Israeli
forces stormed the Askar al-Jadid refugee camp east of Nablus amid live fire and tear gas, though no injuries were reported [1581].
Israeli forces also raided the Zawata area west of Nablus, physically assaulting a young man causing bruises; paramedics also treated
two women experiencing chest pain after Israeli forces surrounded and stormed a residential building in the same area and fully
evacuated it. Additionally, Israeli forces stormed Awarta village south of Nablus with multiple military vehicles, firing tear gas, sound
grenades, and live ammunition, leading to clashes with residents [1623][1631].
On the issue of cultural heritage, in August 2025, Israel announced the classification of 59 archaeological sites in Nablus governorate
as "Israeli archaeological sites," part of a broader listing of 63 additional Palestinian sites across the West Bank. Palestinian observers
also warned that Israeli policies being applied to other West Bank sites could extend to the Tomb of Yusuf in Nablus, currently under
Palestinian administration [1579][1595]. A political analyst noted that settlement expansion pressure is currently concentrated in the
northern West Bank between Nablus, Jenin, and Tulkarm, through the revival and reconstruction of previously dismantled settlements
in a broader and expanded form [1629].
A Palestinian worker from Salem village near Nablus was killed after being shot in the thigh by Israeli forces while attempting to scale
the separation wall near al-Ram, north of Jerusalem, in search of work [1621].
Tulkarm
As of 31 May 2026, Tulkarm Governor Abdullah Kamil announced that Israeli occupation forces had extended their assault on Tulkarm
and Nur Shams refugee camps until 31 July 2026, describing the decision as a further escalation of violations against civilians and a
deepening of the humanitarian crisis for thousands of forcibly displaced families. The governor stated that systematic demolition of
homes and commercial facilities, destruction of infrastructure, and ongoing bulldozing and vandalism inside the camps reflect a
deliberate policy targeting Palestinian communities, exacerbating already severe humanitarian and economic conditions [1602].
Kamil further highlighted that the continued closures imposed on the camps and their surrounding areas deprive residents of basic
rights and deepen the hardship of displaced families. He emphasized that the impacts extend beyond the camps themselves,
affecting citizens' freedom of movement and economic activity across the entire Tulkarm governorate, amid extensive damage to
homes, property, and infrastructure [1602]. He renewed urgent calls on international organizations, humanitarian bodies, and human
rights institutions to intervene to halt the assault and address the ongoing violations against Palestinians in Tulkarm and Nur Shams
camps [1602].
The humanitarian situation in Tulkarm is set against the backdrop of a major Israeli military operation launched in January 2025 in
Tulkarm and Nur Shams refugee camps — coinciding with the Gaza ceasefire — which caused extensive damage to public and private
infrastructure and resulted in large-scale displacement of residents [1607]. The camps have remained under severe access
restrictions, with Jenin Camp similarly maintained as a closed military zone since January 2025, indicating a regional pattern affecting
northern West Bank camps including those in Tulkarm [1607].
The Palestinian Women's Development Association has been conducting legal awareness-raising sessions for women across Tulkarm
governorate, as part of a broader program also covering Nablus, Jenin, and Ramallah governorates. The program consists of 72
sessions, each involving 15 women, aimed at enhancing access to legal protection and services and building capacity to cope with
violence and discrimination [1617]. Separately, a source noted that settlement weight in the northern West Bank is currently
concentrated between Nablus, Jenin, and Tulkarm, through the revival and expansion of settlements, intensifying pressure on
Palestinian communities in the governorate [1629].
Qalqilya
On 29 May 2026, Israeli forces stormed Qalqilya city through its eastern entrance and raided the Nazzal, al-Naqqar, and Juaidi
neighborhoods. According to WAFA, troops detained four Palestinians after searching their homes and ransacking their contents.
During the raid, Israeli forces deployed snipers across the city and posted threatening leaflets before withdrawing after a two-hour
incursion [1577].
Bethlehem
In Bethlehem governorate, Israeli military operations and movement restrictions continued to impose severe humanitarian pressure
during the period of late May 2026. Israeli forces raided the town of al-Khader, south of Bethlehem, where forces deployed across
several neighborhoods, searched homes, and detained a number of residents before later releasing them [1604]. Tighter procedures
at the entrances to Bethlehem townships and around the Etzion settlement complex have been documented, exacerbating the
movement of the population and resulting in partial paralysis of daily life [1586].
A security incident was recorded at the Gush Etzion junction, south of Bethlehem, where Israeli forces killed a Palestinian man following
a reported vehicle ramming attack in which four Israelis were injured, two seriously.
30
The body of the man was subsequently detained by the Israeli occupation authorities [1586].
Regarding settlement expansion, a parallel project referred to as E2, south of Bethlehem, would involve around 2,500 new settlement
units built in a corridor designed to sever the southern West Bank in half, compounding the fragmentation already driven by the E1
project east of Jerusalem [1598]. The Israeli NGO Regavim, which was sanctioned by the European Union, had campaigned for the
demolition of an EU-funded Palestinian primary school in the village of Jabbet al-Dhib, near Bethlehem, illustrating the direct threat to
education infrastructure within the governorate [1622][1625].
On the economic front, Bethlehem University was reported as a site of visible economic distress among young graduates. Nearly 40
percent of young Palestinians in the occupied West Bank holding at least a diploma are unemployed, with overall unemployment
having more than doubled since October 2023, peaking at 35.2 percent in early 2024 and standing at 27.5 percent by end of 2025. The
former mayor of Bethlehem noted that the city is witnessing doctors working in restaurants, nurses struggling to find work, and a
broader exodus of Palestinian talent [1599]. The Palestinian Authority's inability to pay full salaries, driven by Israel's withholding of tax
revenues, has further eroded the public sector as a viable employment pathway [1599].
In a notable community resilience initiative, Aida refugee camp in Bethlehem hosted the fourth edition of its Bazar Return event on the
occasion of Eid al-Adha, after a two-year absence caused by the destruction of the Aida Youth Centre. Thousands of visitors attended
from across Bethlehem district, with participation from dozens of handicraft vendors and cultural performers. Bethlehem Mayor Hanna
Hanania attended and praised the event as a reflection of community cohesion and attachment to the right of return. The head of the
camp's popular services committee noted that the event coincides with the 78th anniversary of the Nakba and that such initiatives
strengthen solidarity and provide families and children with a space for hope despite difficult conditions [1588][1574].
The Church of the Nativity in Bethlehem was identified among the most significant Palestinian archaeological and religious sites
potentially affected by Israeli draft legislation seeking to establish a heritage authority over West Bank sites, which Palestinian and
international observers warn could be used to redefine the historical and cultural identity of these locations [1595].
Jericho
The most directly relevant information for Jericho governorate in the reporting period concerns the Jordan Valley area and the Khan
al-Ahmar Bedouin community, which lies in the E1 corridor east of Jerusalem — a zone that falls within or immediately adjacent to the
Jericho governorate's Area C territory, and is closely linked to the broader Jordan Valley and Dead Sea region referenced in available
sources.
According to OCHA's Humanitarian Situation Report dated 25 May 2026, the Israeli Finance Minister instructed Israeli authorities to
rapidly implement long-standing demolition orders against Khan al-Ahmar, a Bedouin community located east of Jerusalem in the E1
corridor. UNRWA confirmed that Khan al-Ahmar is home to more than 700 Palestine Refugees. The community is one of 18 Bedouin and
herding communities in the path of the E1 settlement expansion plan, with approximately 4,000 Palestinians across these 18
communities at risk of forced displacement. Community leaders reported high levels of fear and uncertainty among residents
following the announcement of the imminent enforcement of demolition orders. A community leader in Khan al-Ahmar described the
decision as "like an electric shock," noting that families had been living under the shadow of demolition orders for years and were
unprepared to leave, with serious concerns about accessing health and education services post-displacement. Since 2009, OCHA has
documented the demolition of approximately 550 structures across the 18 affected communities for lacking Israeli-issued building
permits, including 175 donor-funded structures provided as humanitarian assistance.
On 1 June, Israeli authorities were set to invite bids from private companies to construct 3,400 new settlement units in the E1 area east
of Jerusalem, a step that would effectively cut off occupied East Jerusalem from the rest of the occupied West Bank and further
fragment Palestinian territory. Humanitarian partners have long warned that the E1 settlement plan would heighten the risk of forced
displacement of Bedouin communities and have severe humanitarian consequences.
Also specifically relevant to the Jericho governorate area, on 25 May, the Israeli military, the Israeli Civil Administration, and the Jordan
Valley Regional Council's lands inspection launched a large-scale confiscation operation of vehicles, tractors, and water tanks in firing
zones 900, 901, 902, 903, and 904, stretching across al-Farsiya, al-Jiftlik, Khirbet Hamsa, and Ras el-Ahmar — all communities located in
the northern Jordan Valley within the Jericho governorate. This operation directly affects the livelihoods of Palestinian communities in
the area.
Medical Aid for Palestinians (MAP) reported that its mobile clinics had provided healthcare services to more than 33,000 Palestinians
across 22 communities in the West Bank since 2025, particularly in isolated Area C communities affected by checkpoints, settlement
expansion, and movement restrictions — conditions highly characteristic of communities in the Jericho and Jordan Valley area. Access
to permanent health services in these areas is severely constrained, with mobile care often representing the only healthcare option for
isolated communities.
Jerusalem
During the reporting period of 25 May 2026, OCHA documented several critical protection and displacement incidents specifically
within Jerusalem governorate. On 12 May, Israeli forces opened fire toward two Palestinians attempting to scale the Barrier near
Dahiyat al Bareed, in Jerusalem governorate, killing one Palestinian man and injuring another [1590][1589]. Since 7 October 2023 and
as of 11 May 2026, OCHA has documented the killing of 19 Palestinians and the injury of over 290 others reportedly attempting to cross
the Barrier, a trend directly affecting communities in Jerusalem governorate [1590][1589].
On 14, 15, and 16 May, during the annual Israeli "Jerusalem Day" and "Flag March" events, Israeli settlers and officials marched through
the Old City of Jerusalem and several Palestinian neighbourhoods in East Jerusalem under Israeli forces' protection. Israeli forces
erected barriers and restricted Palestinian movement and access, including to Al-Aqsa Mosque.
31
During these events, settlers assaulted Palestinians and damaged Palestinian-owned property including shops and homes. In the
Silwan neighbourhood, a 16-year-old Palestinian boy was physically assaulted by settlers using a metal stick, sustaining facial
fractures. In the Old City, two Palestinian shop owners were injured after settlers attacked their stores and sprayed them with pepper
spray. According to local sources, Israeli forces also physically assaulted and arrested at least 20 Palestinians across the three-day
events [1590][1589].
Regarding demolitions and displacement directly in East Jerusalem during the reporting period, Israeli authorities demolished
structures resulting in the displacement of 15 people in East Jerusalem as part of a broader operation that displaced 26 people total
across East Jerusalem and Area C [1590][1589]. On 13 May, a particularly significant demolition incident occurred in the Al Marwaha
area of Beit Hanina, on the Jerusalem side of the Barrier, where the Israeli Civil Administration accompanied by Israeli forces
demolished 12 structures in a single operation, including animal shelters, caravans, and storage and sales facilities for construction
materials, affecting seven Palestinian households comprising 41 people including 23 children. The operation resulted in significant
financial losses as affected families were unable to remove most materials and equipment beforehand [1589][1590].
Hundreds of Palestinians living in Area C of eastern Jerusalem governorate are at heightened risk of forced displacement following a
decision by the Israeli Finance Minister instructing Israeli authorities to rapidly implement long-standing demolition orders against
Khan al Ahmar. Bedouin community leaders reported high levels of fear and uncertainty among residents. Khan al Ahmar is among 18
Bedouin and herding communities comprising approximately 4,000 people directly affected by the E1 settlement plan between East
Jerusalem and Ma'ale Adumim settlement [1590][1589]. On 1 June 2026, Israeli authorities were set to invite bids for the construction of
3,400 new settlement units in the E1 area east of Jerusalem, a move that humanitarian partners and international governments have
long warned would further fragment the West Bank and sever East Jerusalem from the rest of the occupied Palestinian territory
[1608][1598]. Since 2009, OCHA has documented the demolition of approximately 550 structures in the 18 affected communities,
including 175 donor-funded structures provided as humanitarian assistance, as permits are nearly impossible for Palestinians to
obtain [1589]. According to Medical Aid for Palestinians (MAP), the community already faces severely disrupted healthcare access due
to movement restrictions and checkpoints, with mobile clinics serving as the primary lifeline for many isolated communities in the area
[1598].
On 27 May 2026, Israeli forces detained a journalist from the Islamic Waqf Department and a woman near Hutta Gate, one of the
entrances to Al-Aqsa Mosque. A journalist was also handed a one-week ban from the mosque compound and required to appear
before Israeli intelligence. In the same reporting period, soldiers raided a house in the town of Anata within Jerusalem governorate,
conducted a thorough search, and detained a resident [1601]. Separately, on 28 May, Israeli forces arrested a woman and a journalist
near Bab Hutta gate leading to Al-Aqsa Mosque, and also raided the home of a young man from Anata northeast of Jerusalem [1604].
On 25 May, in Silwan's al-Bustan neighbourhood in occupied East Jerusalem, three bulldozers accompanied by police began further
demolitions, with 57 of the neighbourhood's 115 homes already reduced to rubble in recent months according to Silwan activists [1592].
Concerning access to Al-Aqsa Mosque, tensions are also heightened by reports of an American-Israeli proposal to challenge
Jordanian guardianship over the site, with Israeli far-right minister Itamar Ben Gvir having visited the compound in April 2026
demanding expanded Jewish access and prayer rights, prompting condemnation from Jordan and the Palestinian Authority [1575].
The UN Secretary-General separately condemned the decision of Israeli authorities to establish Israeli military facilities on the
premises of the UNRWA compound in East Jerusalem, which was expropriated in January 2026, and urged Israel to rescind the decision
and return the Sheikh Jarrah compound to the United Nations [1580].
Access to healthcare in East Jerusalem and surrounding communities remained severely constrained. According to WHO data cited in
a 2025 report, 25% of scheduled appointments in the West Bank were missed due to movement restrictions, with the approval of
permits for medical referrals to East Jerusalem or abroad reduced. Following Israel's ban on UNRWA that came into effect in early 2025,
UNRWA's operations in East Jerusalem were prohibited or severely restricted, including health care, education, and humanitarian
assistance services [1607].
Salfit
According to OCHA's Humanitarian Situation Report dated 25 May 2026, Israeli forces carried out prolonged raids in Salfit governorate
during the reporting period, involving large-scale house searches, temporary takeover of Palestinian homes for military use,
detentions, and reported physical assaults [1589][1590]. Israeli settlers also carried out multiple assaults on Palestinian homes and
infrastructure across Salfit governorate, including physical assaults against Palestinians, attacks on homes while families and children
were inside, damage to water and electricity infrastructure, theft and vandalism of agricultural property, and the destruction of olive
trees and fencing [1589][1590]. A news article from Middle East Eye further reported on the establishment of a settler outpost called
"Ora Yisrael" in Wadi Salfit, deep inside Area B, which prompted settler movement along dirt roads previously used by Palestinian
residents and led to retaliatory attacks against Palestinian Bedouin families in the surrounding area [1585]. Additionally, in August 2025,
Israel announced the inclusion of one Palestinian archaeological site in Salfit on its list of "Israeli archaeological sites," as part of a
broader reclassification affecting thousands of sites across the West Bank, raising concerns about creeping annexation and the
displacement of Palestinian communities from their land [1595].
Tubas
According to OCHA's Humanitarian Situation Report dated 25 May 2026, Israeli forces carried out prolonged raids in multiple
governorates including Tubas, involving large-scale house searches, temporary takeover of Palestinian homes for military use,
detentions, and reported physical assaults [1590][1589]. A specific protection concern in Tubas governorate was also documented in
2025: paramedics in the governorate were reported assaulted by Israeli settlers under the protection of the Israeli Defense Forces,
according to the Safeguarding Health in Conflict Coalition's annual report [1607].
32
A critical development directly affecting connectivity to Tubas was reported around 20 May 2026. Israeli bulldozers demolished
residential structures and animal shelters belonging to a Bedouin shepherd family in Ein el-Hilweh, located in the northern Jordan
Valley. The family's lawyer had previously written to Israeli authorities invoking international law protections against forcible transfer in
occupied territory, but received no response. Soldiers accompanied by a settler also prevented Red Crescent workers from delivering a
tent to the displaced family and confiscated their vehicle. Ein el-Hilweh is described as the last remaining Palestinian community on
Road 5799 — the only road that directly connects the northern Jordan Valley to Tubas — with three surrounding communities already
fully displaced in 2026, representing a severe isolation risk for Tubas through the severing of this key access route [1592].
Gaza Strip
Gaza
The humanitarian situation in Gaza city and the broader Gaza governorate remains catastrophic as of late May 2026, marked by
intensifying military operations, mass displacement, collapsing infrastructure, and acute food and health crises. According to OHCHR
OPT, at least 26 Palestinians were killed across Gaza between 26 and 29 May 2026 — during the Eid al-Adha holiday — including six
women and seven children. Three airstrikes on 26 May alone killed 12 people. One strike hit an apartment in the Al-Rimal
neighbourhood of Gaza City, killing the newly appointed commander of Hamas's Al-Qassam Brigades along with his wife, his sons
aged 15 and 22, his daughter aged 9, and a woman passerby. A separate strike on 27 May in central Gaza City killed at least ten
people, including four girls, one boy, and three women [1663][1675]. Three Palestinians were also killed on a Friday afternoon after an
Israeli drone targeted a civilian gathering at Al-Shawa Square in the Al-Tuffah neighbourhood, east of Gaza City [1642]. An attack on a
police centre in the al-Tawam area north of Gaza City killed six people [1648].
According to Gaza's Ministry of Health, between 12 and 20 May, 24 Palestinians were killed, five bodies were retrieved, two died of
wounds, and 159 were injured across Gaza. The cumulative toll since the ceasefire announced on 10 October 2025 reached 881
fatalities and 2,621 injuries as of 20 May, and subsequently rose to 922 killed and over 2,786 injured by late May, with the overall death
toll since 7 October 2023 reaching nearly 73,000 [1660][1641][1671]. Since the ceasefire, at least 32 children and eight women have been
killed in attacks where fatalities were exclusively women and children [1675][1663].
Between 16 and 17 May, humanitarian partners recorded the displacement of more than 150 families from eastern Gaza city, with
families reporting they fled due to tank movements or bombing. Forty of these families were identified as requiring emergency
assistance, having fled with only what they could carry, and a multi-partner response was initiated [1658][1660]. On 18 May, an airstrike
hit Jabalya Camp, reportedly damaging 35 families' tents and tarpaulins and displacing dozens [1658][1660]. UNRWA reported that
Israeli forces have issued multiple evacuation and shelter-in-place orders in areas including Gaza City, affecting UNRWA installations
including a school-turned-shelter in Nuseirat hosting displaced people [1644].
Israeli Prime Minister Netanyahu announced that Israeli forces control approximately 60% of the Gaza Strip, with a directive to expand
to 70%, which OHCHR OPT warned further contracts the area available to civilians and raises grave concerns about access to
humanitarian assistance [1671][1669][1675]. The Gaza Centre for Human Rights warned that more than 2 million Palestinians are
effectively concentrated within less than 10% of liveable space in Gaza, when accounting for rubble, uninhabitable zones, and militarily
isolated areas [1640]. The so-called "yellow line" and newly imposed buffer zones, including an "orange line," have been pushed
progressively westward, with Palestinian sources indicating Israeli control has extended by an additional 8–9% in recent months
[1643][1636].
Regarding water and sanitation, UNICEF reported as of 29 May 2026 that water availability in Gaza averages only around 6 litres per
person per day — compared to 145–310 litres in the US, Europe, and Israel. For 1.1 million children in Gaza, water remains a daily
uncertainty. Up to 70% of families are unable to collect the minimum 6 litres per person per day, and 82% remain water insecure
[1683][1657]. All water-trucking operations at the Al-Mansoura filling point in Gaza City have been suspended since 17 April following an
incident in which two UNICEF-contracted truck drivers were killed; this filling station is critical for the daily drinking water access of
285,000 people, with UNICEF now trucking water from desalination plants at an additional cost of approximately USD 40,000 per day
[1657]. Seawater desalination output fell from 20,000 m³/day in March to 16,000 m³/day due to chemical and spare part shortages
[1657].
The Sheikh Radwan stormwater lagoon in Gaza City was shut down on 11 May to preserve generator lifespan, causing water and
wastewater levels to rise significantly and raising the risk of flooding and serious public health threats in the coming weeks, according
to the WASH Cluster [1658][1660]. Severe shortages of engine oil are disrupting critical WASH services across Gaza, with the WASH
Cluster estimating approximately 7,000 litres per month are required [1658][1660].
To address pest infestations, WASH Cluster partners and local organizations, in coordination with UNDP, launched a response plan on 17
May targeting over 1,700 locations across Gaza, involving spraying, rodent control, and awareness raising. UNDP brought in 3 tonnes of
rodenticides and 3,000 litres of pesticides, though partners warn that a full response requires Israeli authorities to facilitate access to
Gaza's sanitary landfills, which remain inaccessible due to the presence of Israeli forces [1658][1660]. Displaced families are
increasingly affected by skin infections and illnesses as rats and insects enter shelters and contaminate food [1660].
UNRWA medical surveillance teams continue to report an increase in ectoparasitic infections such as scabies, rodent bites,
chickenpox, and acute watery diarrhoea among both children under five and adults. WHO's representative in the OPT stated that dire
conditions in Gaza — continuing violence, rodent infestations, and spread of infectious disease — are being made worse by blockages
of essential medical supplies, adding that "nobody can replace what UNRWA is doing." Nutrition partners observed a 21% increase in
malnutrition admissions between January and April 2026 compared to the same period in 2025, despite the ceasefire [1644].
33
According to OCHA's 25 May 2026 humanitarian situation report, some 1.7 million people — approximately 354,480 households — are
sheltering in approximately 1,600 displacement sites across Gaza, with nearly 88% residing in makeshift conditions [1658][1660]. Only
half of all aid trucks from Egypt could offload at the Israeli-controlled Kerem Shalom Crossing in the first 18 days of May, with offloading
rates at 81% between 11 and 17 May [1658]. Kerem Shalom and Zikim remain the only operational entry points for humanitarian and
commercial goods [1658][1660].
According to the Gaza Ministry of Health, 250 Palestinians suffering kidney failure risk losing dialysis access, 11,000 diabetic patients
face insulin shortages, and 110 Palestinians with haemophilia are without essential treatment. The healthcare system has seen 76% of
medical imaging equipment destroyed, including all nine MRI units, with only five of 18 CT scanners functioning [1650]. Al-Aqsa Martyrs
Hospital in Deir al-Balah — the only government hospital in the central Gaza Strip, serving over half a million residents and displaced
persons — faces imminent generator failure due to high wear and critical shortages of spare parts, with the hospital's administration
appealing to international institutions for an immediate and permanent electricity solution [1664].
On food security, WFP confirmed that approximately 1.6 million people in Gaza — around 77% of the population — are currently suffering
from high levels of acute food insecurity. The World Central Kitchen (WCK) implemented a severe austerity package due to a funding
crisis, dismissing over 500 employees, reducing daily cooked meals from 1 million to 200,000 (an 80% drop), and closing numerous
community kitchens and distribution points. This directly impacts over 250,000 displaced families who depend on these meals as a
sole food source. In Deir al-Balah, massive popular protests erupted in front of WCK distribution headquarters, with hundreds of
displaced families, including widows, orphans, and elderly people, denouncing the aid cuts [1665]. One in five families in Gaza is eating
only one snack per full day, with mothers reducing their own rations to feed their children [1665]. The UN's 2026 flash appeal seeks USD
4 billion for 3 million Palestinians in Gaza and the West Bank but has received only 12% of the required amount (approximately USD 490
million) to date [1665].
An airstrike on 29 May hit a residential area near five humanitarian facilities in Deir al-Balah in central Gaza, with no casualties
reported; this followed an Israeli military order to shelter in place shortly before the strike. Between 11 and 17 May, mental health and
psychosocial support services were provided to more than 10,000 people in shelters, camps, schools, and displacement sites, though
OCHA warned that continuing these services — particularly for children — requires fuel, safe spaces, staff, and basic resources [1675].
In Deir al-Balah, an airstrike hit a residential square near Al-Aqsa Martyrs Hospital following an evacuation warning, destroying two
houses and causing extensive damage to nearby homes, mosques, and civilian vehicles [1653]. In Khan Younis, Israeli aircraft shelled
agricultural land and a vegetable market following an eviction warning, causing extensive destruction and injuries, and a separate
strike hit a car in Al-Mawasi, Khan Younis, killing two men on 26 May [1653][1663].
North Gaza
On 12 May 2026, in Beit Lahia city within North Gaza governorate, an area near an MSF team was struck by two shells fired from an
Israeli tank, injuring at least 12 people. The impact occurred approximately 400 metres from Al Tayeb Clinic, according to MSF East
Africa [1679].
Israeli forces also launched strikes east of Jabalia refugee camp in northern Gaza at dawn during the Eid al-Adha period, with artillery
shelling reported in the area [1662]. During the same period, an airstrike hit Jabalya Camp on 18 May, reportedly damaging 35 families'
tents and tarpaulins and displacing dozens of them [1658][1660].
Separately, a Palestinian was shot by Israeli forces in the Salatin area west of Beit Lahia town in the north of the Strip [1636]. According
to reports, others were shot at in this area as part of ongoing Israeli military activity targeting civilians in the northern part of the Strip
[1636].
According to the CARE IN THE CROSSHAIRS 2025 report by the Safeguarding Health in Conflict Coalition, access to health care in North
Gaza was severely affected by the IDF's use of explosives-laden robots deployed near health facilities in Jabalia refugee camp, as well
as gun drones used near health facilities and ambulances [1676]. By the end of 2025, there were no functioning hospitals in North Gaza
governorate — a complete absence that formed part of a broader collapse of Gaza's health system [1676]. This critical gap forced
patients to rely on overwhelmed field hospitals established by international organizations, which themselves faced severe shortages of
medical supplies, equipment, and fuel [1676].
Access constraints were particularly acute in northern Gaza throughout 2025, where attacks, displacement, and siege-like conditions
severely limited access to health care and other essential services [1676]. The IDF's use of explosives-laden robots near Jabalia
refugee camp further obstructed health care access for the local population [1676].
An attack on a police centre in the al-Tawam area, north of Gaza City, killed six people, with bodies reportedly found in a state of
advanced mutilation and burns, further illustrating the extreme vulnerability of civilian infrastructure and personnel in the northern part
of the Strip [1648]. A media report also described a four-year-old girl with broken arms whose parents were killed in a raid in the north
of the sector, left to walk alone in the streets [1655].
Deir Al-Balah
During the period around Eid al-Adha (late May 2026), Deir al-Balah in central Gaza experienced a sharp escalation in violence,
displacement, and humanitarian deterioration. According to UNRWA Situation Report #223 (covering 20–25 May 2026), Israeli forces
issued multiple evacuation and shelter-in-place orders in areas west of the Israeli-militarised "Yellow Line," explicitly including Deir
al-Balah [1644].
On Saturday, 31 May 2026, an Israeli drone strike targeting a group of civilians near Al-Aqsa Martyrs Hospital in Deir al-Balah killed a
doctor who served as head of the anaesthesia department at Al-Yafa Medical Hospital in central Gaza, and injured three others
including a child [1689][1688].
34
Arab News and Middle East Monitor reported this strike as a direct hit on the city of Deir al-Balah, with Israeli warplanes carrying out the
attack [1641]. Separately, OCHA reported that on approximately 29 May (Thursday), an airstrike hit a residential area near five
humanitarian facilities in Deir al-Balah, with no casualties reported but preceded by an Israeli military shelter-in-place order [1675].
Also in the days surrounding Eid al-Adha, an aerial attack struck a residential area near Al-Aqsa Martyrs Hospital following an
evacuation warning, destroying two houses, causing extensive damage to nearby homes, mosques, and civilian vehicles, and resulting
in injuries among the population [1653]. Al-Aqsa Martyrs Hospital, the only government hospital in the central governorate serving
more than half a million residents and displaced persons from Gaza and the north, issued urgent warnings about potential generator
shutdowns due to severe shortages of spare parts and high equipment wear, directly threatening the lives of hundreds of sick and
wounded patients [1664].
Residents east of Deir al-Balah were also forcibly displaced by Israeli-backed armed militias operating near the Yellow Line. A resident
from east of Deir al-Balah described how armed men affiliated with these militias entered the area and gave families until 10pm to
evacuate their homes, taking only what they could carry, resulting in the near-complete emptying of the area [1681].
The humanitarian situation for the population sheltering in Deir al-Balah is severe. Displaced families from northern Gaza have been
sheltering in the city, living in tents in conditions of extreme deprivation. Residents described being unable to afford basic goods —
including new clothing for children — with the cost of a sacrificial lamb reaching approximately 13,000 shekels (around $5,000) during
Eid al-Adha [1668][1685]. A displaced resident living in a tent west of Deir al-Balah described ongoing displacement, anxiety, and the
absence of any of the pre-war holiday traditions [1685].
In response to the funding crisis facing World Central Kitchen (WCK), which cut its daily meals in Gaza by 80% — from 1 million to
200,000 meals — Deir al-Balah witnessed large popular protests and sit-ins in front of WCK distribution headquarters. Hundreds of
displaced workers, widows, orphans, and elderly people participated, raising fears about the return of severe famine conditions
experienced before the October 2025 ceasefire [1665].
According to UNICEF's WASH Situation Update dated 29 May 2026, UNICEF implemented a pilot household-level waste segregation
initiative in four shelters specifically in Deir al-Balah and Al-Zawayda, achieving approximately a 15–20 per cent reduction in overall
waste volume in those shelters [1683]. This initiative reflects the acute solid waste and sanitation crisis affecting displacement sites in
the area, where displaced families are increasingly affected by skin infections and illnesses as rats and insects enter shelters and
contaminate food [1658][1660].
Regarding the broader health care situation, the "Care in the Crosshairs" 2025 report noted that by 2025, attacks on health care in Deir
al-Balah had been reducing compared to prior years, though the governorate had previously seen high concentrations of explosive
drone incidents in 2024 [1676]. Despite this relative reduction, the Al-Aqsa Martyrs Hospital remains the sole government hospital for
the entire central governorate and continues to face critical operational risks from infrastructure degradation and supply shortages
[1664].
Khan Younis
Between 16 and 17 May 2026, humanitarian partners recorded the displacement of more than 150 families from eastern Khan Younis,
with affected families reporting they fled due to tank movements or bombing. According to OCHA, 40 of the newly displaced families
were identified as requiring emergency assistance, having fled with only what they could carry, and a multi-partner response was
initiated [1658][1660][1648].
Israeli forces also demolished residential buildings in eastern Khan Younis throughout the week of 19–25 May, compounding
displacement pressures in the area [1650]. Israeli artillery shelling targeted areas east and south of Khan Younis city on the morning of
31 May, while a separate artillery strike targeted the eastern neighbourhoods of Khan Younis around the same period [1689][1687].
During the Eid al-Adha period (around 26–27 May), multiple lethal incidents were recorded in Khan Younis. On 26 May, an Israeli
airstrike hit a car in al-Mawasi, Khan Younis, killing two men, according to OHCHR [1669][1671]. A separate Israeli strike on a vehicle in
Khan Younis killed two Palestinians, and separately, Israeli naval vessels opened fire toward the coast of Khan Younis, with no
immediate reported casualties [1686][1693]. An Israeli airstrike on an internally displaced persons camp in Mawasi Khan Younis killed a
woman and a girl, and injured more than 20 people; a girl later died of her wounds [1653]. In the al-Maslakh al-Turki area of Khan
Younis, a Palestinian woman was injured by Israeli gunfire in the early hours of the day as tents sheltering displaced people came
under fire, with the injured taken to Nasser Hospital [1686][1693]. Additionally, on 25 May, a 14-year-old girl died of serious injuries
sustained in an Israeli bombing of the Mawasi Khan Younis area, having been among those wounded in a strike on internally displaced
persons' tents at the "Gith" camp in the Mawasi area west of Khan Younis, where the same shelling killed another girl and a woman and
injured 30 others, most of them children and women, according to sources cited by Anadolu Agency [1686][1655][1677][1690].
In the agricultural domain, Israeli forces shelled agricultural land and a vegetable market in Khan Younis following an evacuation
warning, causing extensive destruction and injuries [1653]. The agricultural lands and greenhouses of Khan Younis, which once
supplied more than 40 percent of the entire Strip's daily domestic food needs, have been largely destroyed; the UN Food and
Agriculture Organization (FAO) reports that less than five percent of Gaza's agricultural land remains usable. In the Shakoush area,
Israeli bulldozers razed greenhouses and confiscated topsoil, directly exacerbating food insecurity [1652]. A Palestinian journalist in
Khan Younis described scenes of people returning from soup kitchens with empty pots and many families unable to secure any food,
warning that a famine could emerge at any moment [1652].
Hamad City in Khan Younis, a residential complex built with Qatari funding comprising 53 buildings and approximately 3,000 housing
units that previously housed over 15,000 people — primarily low-income families displaced by previous conflicts — has been reduced
to rubble, surrounded by displaced families [1652]. The Sheikh Mohammed cemetery in the Maan area of Khan Younis has been erased
35
and replaced by an Israeli military outpost, according to satellite imagery updated to February 25, 2026 [1652].
In terms of humanitarian response, UNRWA on 20 May began operating a new Temporary Learning Space (TLS) at a displacement site
south of Khan Younis — the first TLS established in a location without any operational UNRWA installations. Within one week, 1,600
children registered in the new TLS, which is made up of multiple tents [1644]. Partners also installed 115 emergency shelters in Gaza and
Khan Younis using Shelter Cluster Emergency Shelter Kit designs and specifications between 11 and 17 May [1658][1660].
Displaced residents in Khan Younis city continue to live in torn tents inside overcrowded shelters, with reports of families — including
widows who lost their breadwinners — struggling to secure food amid the collapse of livelihoods and health services [1667]. In Khan
Younis, residents gathered for Eid al-Adha prayers amid destroyed buildings, with displaced persons describing conditions of ongoing
human suffering and loss [1680][1691]. A resident of Khan Younis described the situation starkly, questioning where people were
expected to go given there was no room left [1684].
Rafah
As of late May 2026, Rafah governorate remains one of the most severely affected areas in the Gaza Strip, with its urban landscape
largely erased following sustained Israeli military operations. According to Al Jazeera, satellite imagery confirms that southern Rafah
has been almost entirely flattened, with only faint outlines of streets visible amid debris. The Swedish village in Rafah — a coastal
community of roughly 1,300 people founded in 1965 with international assistance to shelter Palestinian refugees and historically
dependent on fishing — has been systematically destroyed and converted into an Israeli military outpost, with only five houses left
standing [1652].
The Rafah border crossing, previously the sole lifeline connecting Gaza to Egypt, has had its civilian infrastructure — including
passenger departure and arrival halls, a VIP terminal, a humanitarian logistics facility, and administrative offices — replaced by heavily
fortified Israeli military observation posts and razor wire [1652]. Between 11 and 17 May 2026, partners supported the medical
evacuation of 59 patients, including six children, to Egypt via Rafah Crossing, alongside 87 caregivers, indicating the crossing retains
limited humanitarian functionality [1658][1660]. Since the reopening of the Rafah border and as of 14 May, 2,429 returnees from Egypt
have been assisted by protection partners, with 885 currently receiving follow-up protection services [1660][1658].
The agricultural lands and greenhouses of Rafah and Khan Younis formerly constituted Gaza's food basket, producing the vast
majority of the territory's fresh vegetables and supplying more than 40 percent of the Strip's daily domestic food needs. According to
the UN Food and Agriculture Organization (FAO), less than five percent of Gaza's agricultural land remains usable, and in the Shakoush
area Israeli bulldozers have razed greenhouses and confiscated topsoil, directly exacerbating food insecurity [1652]. Israeli battleships
were also reported firing at the coast of Rafah and Khan Younis during the reporting period, though no injuries were reported in that
specific incident [1686].
By the end of 2025, the Rafah governorate had a complete absence of functioning hospitals, with no hospitals operational in either
North Gaza or Rafah, according to a 2025 report by the Safeguarding Health in Conflict Coalition [1676]. The collapse of health
infrastructure, combined with mass displacement, overcrowded makeshift shelters, poor sanitation, and restricted medical supply
access, has created severe public health risks in southern Gaza including the spread of acute respiratory infections, hepatitis A, skin
conditions, and mental health disorders [1676]. Solid waste management in southern Gaza continues to rely on temporary dump sites
located near active displacement sites, with access to sanitary landfills near Gaza's perimeter blocked due to Israeli forces'
deployment [1658]. Partners indicate that a full pest-control response requires Israeli authorities to facilitate access to Gaza's landfills
where waste can be safely disposed of [1658][1660]. Displaced families are increasingly affected by skin infections and other illnesses
as rats and insects enter shelters and contaminate food [1658].
